An existence and uniqueness theory to stochastic partial differential equations involving pseudo-differential operators driven by space-time white noise Thanks: The authors were supported by the National Research Foundation of Korea(NRF) grant funded by the Korea government(MSIT) (No.2020R1A2C1A01003959)
Jae-Hwan Choi, Ildoo Kim

Abstract
. In this paper, we aim to develop a new weak formulation that ensures well-posedness for a broad range of stochastic partial differential equations with pseudo-differential operators whose symbols depend only on time and spatial frequencies. The main focus of this paper is to relax the conditions on the symbols of pseudo-differential operators and data while still ensuring that the stochastic partial differential equations remain well-posed in a weak sense. Specifically, we allow symbols to be random and remove all regularity and ellipticity conditions on them. As a result, our main operators include many interesting rough operators that cannot generate any regularity gain or integrability improvement from the equations. In addition, our data do not need to be regular or possess finite stochastic moments.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60H15, 35S10, 35D30

1.
Introduction

Research on stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) is now recognized as a significant field in mathematics, given its numerous intriguing applications across various sciences and the challenging mathematical problems it presents.
Much of the research in this area has focused on solving specific equations originating from scientific disciplines.
For instance, extensive studies have been dedicated to filtering equations, KPZ equations, Anderson models, stochastic fluid dynamics, population dynamics, financial mathematics, and other related topics (
cf
.
[
54
,
60
]
and references therein).

The irregular effects of random noises typically hinder the derivation of these theories from (deterministic) partial differential equations (PDEs). As a result, many SPDEs are considered ill-posed within the traditional PDE framework.
To overcome this challenge, new concepts have been developed to provide precise mathematical meanings for solutions of various SPDEs.
Consequently, a broad array of mathematical tools are now available for studying these equations.
For instance, these tools include variational methods with monotone operators (
[
59
,
30
,
51
]
), random field approach (
[
64
,
50
]
), semi-group methods (
[
16
,
56
]
),
L_{p}
-analytic approach (
[
31
]
),

H^{\infty}
-calculus (
[
57
,
58
]
), regularity structures (
[
24
,
26
]
), paracontrolled calculus (
[
25
]
), stochastic Calderón-Zygmund theory (
[
45
]
), scaling limit approach (
[
4
]
), and vector-valued harmonic analysis methods (
[
52
,
53
]
).

Most research focuses on solving SPDEs that are relevant to scientific applications and most mathematical models are simplified with nice operators and data. Consequently, these studies strive to understand solutions and equations in a practical sense rather than merely exploring possible mathematical generalizations.
As a result, the main operators used in SPDEs, such as the Laplacian operator, the fractional Laplacian operator, and diffusion operators, are highly regular and well-behaved.
These operators enhance the regularity of the solutions, enabling us to comprehend the equations and solutions in a robust manner, even when the input data is relatively rough due to randomness.

However, historically, there had been attempts to solve PDEs with general operators that do not generate regularity gains in weak formulations, particularly after Schwartz’s distribution theory became prominent. Mathematicians discovered long ago that any linear partial differential equation with constant coefficients, such as

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}u(x)=f(x)\quad x\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

(1.1)

is solvable for any distribution
f
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Here,
\alpha
is a multi-index,
c_{\alpha}
is a complex number, and the summation is finite.
For more details, see
[
28
,
29
]
and references therein.

We provide additional details with some heuristics from the aforementioned references. Formally, by taking the Fourier transform of both sides of (
1.1
), we get

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)=\mathcal{F}[f](\xi)\quad x\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

(1.2)

where the symbol
\psi(\xi)
is given by
\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}\mathrm{i}^{|\alpha|}\xi^{\alpha}
.

Due to the Fourier inversion theorem, it suffices to solve (
1.2
) instead of (
1.1
).
Since
\psi(\xi)
is a polynomial, both sides of (
1.2
) can be understood as weak functionals on a class of test functions according to the multiplication action,
i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\psi\mathcal{F}[u],\varphi\rangle:=\langle\mathcal{F}[u],\psi\varphi\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}[f],\varphi\rangle,

(1.3)

where
\varphi
is a test function and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes a duality paring.

It is important to note that (
1.2
) loses the uniqueness property of a solution even as linear functionals unless the symbol
\psi
satisfies additional conditions such as ellipticity and polynomial growth.
It is naturally expected since if both the symbol
\psi
and data
\mathcal{F}[f]
are zero on a domain, then any value of
\mathcal{F}[u]
on this domain is enough to be a solution.
The breakdown of the uniqueness also can be comprehended through the following simple example.
For any real number
c
, consider two functions
u_{1}(x)=e^{cx}
and
u_{2}(x)=e^{-cx}
.
Then both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
become solutions to
the equation

\displaystyle u^{\prime\prime}(x)-c^{2}u(x)=0\quad x\in\mathbf{R}.

At a glimpse, it also looks impossible that there exists a weak solution to (
1.2
) since
if the symbol
\psi
is zero but data
\mathcal{F}[f]
is non-zero in a domain, then any value of
\mathcal{F}[u]
in this domain cannot satisfy the equation. However, if
\psi
is not identically zero, then the Lebesgue measure of the set of all zeros of
\psi
,
\psi^{-1}(0):=\{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:\psi(\xi)=0\}
, becomes
0
since
\psi
is a non-zero polynomial. Thus, (
1.2
) is solvable in a weak sense and numerous methods had been developed to find solutions to (
1.2
) in a weak sense, thanks to the contributions of Ehrenpreis, Malgrange, Bernshtejn, Gel’fand, Hörmander, Lojasiewicz, and others.
This problem is called “the division problem” and was successfully solved a long time ago even though the uniqueness fails to hold.

This issue seems to extend to pseudo-differential operators by considering various symbols rather than just polynomials.
In other words, one can consider the equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\psi(x,\xi)\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)\right](x)=f(x)\quad x\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

(1.4)

which is derived from (
1.2
) and the inverse Fourier transform.
Perturbing these types of operators with respect to
x
without a very strong assumption is nearly impossible.
Therefore, we specifically consider the special case where the symbol
\psi
is independent of
x
, since we aim to find a weaker condition on
\psi
as possible.

Nonetheless, the equation

\displaystyle\psi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(x):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\psi(\xi)\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)\right](x)=f(x)\quad x\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

(1.5)

is still impossible to solve even in a weak sense unless there exists a strong mathematical assumption on
\psi
.
Recall that all these classical theories were feasible since our symbol
\psi
is a polynomial,
i.e.
the main operator in (
1.1
) is a merely (partial) differential operator with constant coefficients.
In particular, the multiplication action
\varphi\to\psi\varphi
in (
1.6
) is well-defined since
\psi
is a polynomial.
Additionally, the uniqueness of a solution does not even hold for polynomial symbols.
Therefore, it is impossible to find a unique weak solution to (
1.5
) with a non-polynomial symbol unless strong conditions such as smoothness, polynomial growth, and uniform ellipticity appear (
cf
.
[
34
, Chapters 12 and 13]
).
In other words, (
1.5
) is generally ill-posed.

However, if we reconsider the equation in the form of (
1.2
) by ignoring the inverse Fourier transform in (
1.5
), it might seem solvable without smoothness on symbols if the set of all zeros of
\psi
is a null set.
Nonetheless, uniqueness is still unattainable without additional strong conditions.
This vague idea might be developed into a rigorous mathematical theory, but to the best of our knowledge, no suitable references address this with non-regular symbols.

As a broader extension, one can also consider an evolutionary generalization of (
1.5
) with a family of pseudo differential operators
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
as follows:

\displaystyle u_{t}(t,x)=\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x)+f(t,x)\quad(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},

(1.6)

where
T\in(0,\infty]
,

\displaystyle\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x):=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}\left[\psi(t,\xi)\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right](x)

(1.7)

and both
d
-dimensional Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}
are taken only with respect to the space variable.

It might seem that our evolutionary equations are more complicate than stationary equations such as (
1.5
).
However, if our symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
is merely given by a polynomial only with respect to
\xi
(uniformly for all
t
), then the equation (
1.6
) can be viewed as a special case of (
1.5
) (and thus a simple particular case of (
1.4
)) on
\mathbf{R}^{d+1}
by extending the symbol for all
(t,\xi)\in(-\infty,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}=\mathbf{R}^{d+1}
.

Consequently, there exists a distribution solution
u
to (
1.6
) by applying classical theories on the
(d+1)
-dimensional Euclidean space if
\psi(t,\xi)=\sum_{\alpha}c^{\alpha}\mathrm{i}^{|\alpha|}\xi^{\alpha}
and
f
is a distribution on
\mathbf{R}^{d+1}
, where the summation is finite.

Moreover, we can even address SPDEs driven by space-time white noise, since this random noise can be interpreted as inhomogeneous data
f
given by tempered distributions. However, classical theories do not guarantee the uniqueness of a weak solution, even for this uniformly well-behaved polynomial symbol. Additionally, there is no comprehensive theory for (
1.4
) if
\psi
lacks smoothness.

In conclusion, it might be impossible to show the existence and uniqueness of a weak solution to (
1.6
) if the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
varies with respect to the time and lacks regularity and ellipticity.

Surprisingly, we recently discovered a unique solution to (
1.6
) in a weak sense, despite the symbols being non-elliptic and non-smooth (
[
15
]
).
Notably, the symbols can take on any values in the complex number system, which causes certain functions to display exponential growth.
Specifically, the real parts of these symbols can simultaneously include zeros, positive, and negative values.
This violates the conditions for any ellipticity and allows the kernels of (
1.6
) to exhibit exponential growth at infinity.
We succinctly describe these properties by saying that our symbols (or pseudo-differential operators) are
sign-changing
.

The key idea behind this result involves using a special class of test functions that contain an approximation of the identity and extending the domain of the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms to a larger class than that of all tempered distributions on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Interestingly, this new class is neither the class of all distributions in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
nor does it have an inclusion relation with it. However, the new class and the class of all distributions are homeomorphic due to the newly extended Fourier transform. More details will be provided in the following section.

This new finding has prompted us to pursue further research. Specifically, our new primary objective is to investigate whether these novel concepts can be applied to SPDEs involving pseudo-differential operators driven by space-time white noise. We are writing this paper because we realized that all these ideas can indeed be extended to such SPDEs. A more precise mathematical formulation will be presented in the next section.

We conclude this introduction with a few thoughts. We are confident that our theory offers numerous advantages, even from an application standpoint, as many generators of two-parameter semi-groups can be expressed in the form of the operator in (
1.7
).
Specifically, many generators of Markov processes can be represented by these operators.

However, beyond practical applications, we view our research as a mathematical challenge in its own right. Our goal is to solve as general equations as possible, driven by the following question:

What is the most general operator that maintains well-posedness for linear SPDEs in a weak sense?

Although we cannot claim that our results provide the ultimate answer to this question, we believe they are quite remarkable.
Our findings show that all SPDEs involving pseudo-differential operators are well-posed in a certain weak sense, even though the symbols are irregular, exhibit exponential growth at infinity, lack any elliptic condition, and even are stochastic.

At last, we specify the notation used in the article.

•

Let
\mathbf{N}
,
\mathbf{Z}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{C}
denote the natural number system, the integer number system, the real number system, and the complex number system, respectively. For
d\in\mathbf{N}
,
\mathbf{R}^{d}
denotes the
d
-dimensional Euclidean space.

•

For
i=1,...,d
, a multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{d})
with

\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1,2,...\}
, and a function
g
, we set

\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{i}}=D_{x^{i}}g,\quad D^{\alpha}g=D_{x^{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot...\cdot D^{\alpha_{d}}_{x^{d}}g,\quad|\alpha|:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}.

•

We use
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
or
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
to denote the space of all infinitely differentiable (complex-valued) functions with compact supports.

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
represents the Schwartz space in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and the topology on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is generated by the Schwartz semi-norms
\sup_{x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}}|x^{\alpha}(D^{\beta}f)(x)|
for all multi-indexes
\alpha
and
\beta
.

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is used to denote the dual space of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
i.e.

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is the space of all tempered distributions on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Additionally, we assume that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is a topological space equipped with the weak*-topology if there is no special remark about the topology on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

•

Let
(X,\mathcal{M},\mu)
be a measure space.
Assume that
f(t)
and
g(t)
are complex-valued
\mathcal{M}
-measurable functions on
X
.
We write

\displaystyle f(x)=g(x)\quad(a.e.)~x\in X

(1.8)

or say that for almost every
x\in X
,
f
and
g
are equal
iff there exists a measurable subset
\mathcal{T}\subset X
such that the measure
\mu(X\setminus\mathcal{T})
is zero and
f(x)=g(x)
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
Moreover, we say that a function
f(t)
is defined
(a.e.)
on a
X
if there exists a measurable subset
\mathcal{T}\subset X
such that the measure
\mu(X\setminus\mathcal{T})=0
and
f(t)
is defined for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
Especially, if the measure
\mu
is a probability measure,
i.e.

\mu(X)=1
, then we use
(a.s.)
instead of
(a.e.)
.
Furthermore, we also say that
f(x)=g(x)
with probability one instead of (
1.8
) if
\mu
is a probability measure.

•

For
R>0
,

B_{R}(x):=\{y\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:|x-y|<R\}.

•

For a measurable function
f
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, we denote the
d
-dimensional Fourier transform of
f
by

\mathcal{F}[f](\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot x}f(x)\mathrm{d}x

and the
d
-dimensional inverse Fourier transform of
f
by

\mathcal{F}^{-1}[f](x):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi}f(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi.

We refer to
\xi
as the frequency of
f
.

Moreover, for a function
f(t,x)
defined on
(0,T)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, we use the notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{x}[f](\xi):=\mathcal{F}[f(t,x)](\xi):=\mathcal{F}[f(t,\cdot)](\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\xi\cdot x}f(t,x)\mathrm{d}x

and it is called the Fourier transform of
f
with respect to the space variable.
Additionally, we say that
\xi
is a frequency with respect to the space variable.
In particular, we sometimes use the terminology “frequency function” instead of the Fourier transform.

On the other hand, for the inverse Fourier transform of
f(t,\xi)
with respect to the space variable, we use the notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}[f](x):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[f(t,\xi)](x):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[f(t,\cdot)](x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}e^{\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi}f(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi.

More general Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms acting on linear functionals are discussed in Section
2
.
To simplify notation, we typically omit the subscripts indicated by
x
and
\xi
.

•

We write
\alpha\lesssim\beta
if there is a positive constant
N
such that
\alpha\leq N\beta
.

•

For
z\in\mathbf{C}
,
\Re[z]
denotes the real part of
z
,
\Im[z]
is the imaginary part of
z
, and
\bar{z}
is the complex conjugate of
z
.

2.
Settings and function classes

We fix a finite stopping time
\tau
and
d\in\mathbf{N}
throughout the paper.
Here,
\tau
and
d
denote the (random) terminal time of the equation and the dimension of the space-variable, respectively.

Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},P)
be a complete probability space,

\mathscr{G}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field of
\mathscr{F}
, and
\mathscr{H}
be a sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
, where
where
\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
denote the Borel sets on
[0,\infty)
(generated by the Euclidean norms).

\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{H}
provide measurability information for an initial data
u_{0}
and a deterministic inhomogeneous data
f
, respectively.
Note that
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{H}
can be arbitrary sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
, respectively.
For notational convenience, we fix

\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{F}
throughout the paper, but it is important to note that all results can be applied to different sub-
\sigma
-algebras due to their generality.

Additionally, let
\{\mathscr{F}_{t},t\geq 0\}
be an increasing filtration of

\sigma
-fields
\mathscr{F}_{t}\subset\mathscr{F}
, each of which contains all

(\mathscr{F},P)
-null sets. By
\mathcal{P}
we denote the predictable

\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{\mathscr{F}_{t},t\geq 0\}
and we assume that
on
\Omega
there exist independent one-dimensional Wiener
processes (Brownian motions)
B^{1}_{t},B^{2}_{t},...
, each of which is a Wiener
process relative to
\{\mathscr{F}_{t},t\geq 0\}
.

Now we consider a complex-valued
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function
\psi(t,\xi)
defined on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
and denote

\displaystyle\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\psi(t,\cdot)\mathcal{F}[u](t,\cdot)\right](x)

(2.1)

in the whole paper, where
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
denotes the Borel sets on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
generated by the standard Euclidean norm.
The operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
is called a (time-measurable) pseudo-differential operator
and the function
\psi(t,\xi)
is called
the symbol
of the operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
.

We will discuss the domain of the operator
f\mapsto\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)f
and properties of the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
to ensure the operator is well-defined later.
In simple terms, the operator can be defined based on the identity from Plancherel’s theorem, which is provided in Definition
2.7
below.
It is important to note that our symbol can be random, sign-changing, and does not require any regularity condition.

In this paper, we study the existence and uniqueness of a weak solution
u
to the SPDEs with the pseudo-differential operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
driven by space-time white noise as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u=\left(\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x)+f(t,x)\right)\mathrm{d}t+h(t,x)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\quad

\displaystyle(t,x)\in(0,\tau]\times\mathbf{R}^{d},

\displaystyle u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\quad

\displaystyle x\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

(2.2)

where
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
denotes a space-time white noise,
i.e.

\mathbf{B}_{t}
is a cylindrical Wiener process on
L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

The equation conceals the sample point
\omega\in\Omega
as it typically does.
Here, our solutions are weak with respect to an analytic point of view,
i.e.
a solution
u
satisfies (
2.2
)
as a linear functional (complex-valued linear function) defined on a class of test functions.
This class of test functions is selected as a dense subspace of the Schwartz class, as defined in Definition
2.1
, rather than the usual
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
or the Schwartz class itself, to manage the positive part of the symbol
\psi
. The precise definition of our solution is provided in Definition
3.9
.
Furthermore, it is important to note that our solutions are stochastically strong in the sense that we solve (
2.2
) after fixing a probability space where noises
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
can be constructed.

It is well-known that the space-white noise
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
has a representation so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}=\eta_{k}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{k},

(2.3)

where
\{\eta_{k}:k\in\mathbf{N}\}
is an orthonomal basis of
L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\{B^{k}_{t}:k\in\mathbf{N}\}
is a sequence of independent one-dimensional (real-valued) Brownian motions.
In particular, we fix an orthonomal basis
\{\eta_{k}:k\in\mathbf{N}\}
so that
\eta_{k}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
;
This type of orthonomal basis can be easily constructed based on the Hermite polynomials.
We consider the space-time white noise constructed from (
2.3
) throughout the paper.
Due to this construction, at least formally, the term
h(t,x)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
is equal to

h(t,x)\eta_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{k}
, where Einstein’s summation convention is used,
i.e.
the summation notation is hidden for the repeated index, which implies

\displaystyle h(t,x)\eta_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{k}=h(t,x)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{k}.

This connection naturally raises the necessity of handling sequence-valued functions and stochastic processes.
Specifically,
l_{2}
-valued stochastic processes are importantly treated to classify stochastic inhomogeneous data, where

l_{2}
denotes the class of all (complex-valued) sequences
a=\left(a^{1},a^{2},a^{3},\ldots\right)
such that

\displaystyle|a|_{l_{2}}:=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|a^{k}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

Recall that our main goal is to find the weakest possible conditions on the symbol
\psi
and data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
h
to ensure the existence of a unique weak solution to (
2.2
).
Especially, our goal is to eliminate all regularity conditions on the symbol
\psi
and the data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
h
.
More specifically, we seek appropriate local integrability conditions on the symbol
\psi
and data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
h
with respect to the spatial frequencies to make (
2.2
) well-posed.
All functions in the paper are complex-valued if there is no special mention about ranges of functions.

We present our main assumptions on the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
in the next section to ensure our weak well-posedness.
Initially, we discuss defining our main operator
\psi(t,\xi)
without imposing strong assumptions on
\psi
.
The operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
is defined using the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms, as shown in (
2.1
).
Thus, it is necessary to consider the broadest possible domain for these transforms if we aim to relax the conditions on the symbol
\psi
ensuring that the operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
is well-defined.

It is well-known that the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms are automorphisms on the class of all tempered distributions, where a tempered distribution is a (complex-valued) continuous linear functional defined on the

d
-dimensional Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Additionally, the domain of the Fourier transform can be extended to include all distributions, aided by the Paley-Wiener theorem (
cf.

[
29
, Proposition 4.1]
). This extension relies on analytic continuations and is therefore only suitable for smooth symbols
\psi
.
Consequently, this extension is not useful for defining the operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
since it requires the smoothness of the symbol
\psi
, which contradicts our main objective.

Fortunately, we discovered an alternative method to extend the domain of the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms to include all distributions, as suggested by the authors in
[
15
]
. We provide more precise definitions of the extended Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms below.

Definition 2.1

(Fourier transforms of distributions)
.

We use
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
to denote the subclass of the Schwartz class whose Fourier transform is in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d}):=\{u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d}):\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\},

where
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
represents the (complex-valued) Schwartz class on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is the class of all complex-valued infinitely differentiable functions with compact supports defined on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
The Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is equipped with the strong topology generated by Schwartz’s semi-norms. Since
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is a subset of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, it inherits the subspace topology. We can then consider the dual space of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, denoted as
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
i.e.

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

if and only if
u
is a continuous linear functional defined on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
For
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, we write

\displaystyle\langle u,\varphi\rangle:=u(\varphi).

In other words,
\langle u,\varphi\rangle
denotes the image of
\varphi
under
u
.
We adopt the weak*-topology for
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and consider the Borel sets generated by this topology.
We call
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
the space of all
the Fourier transforms of distributions
. The rationale behind this naming and the notation will be explained in Definition
2.3
below.

Similarly, we define the space of
l_{2}
-valued continuous linear functions on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
This space is denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
and consists of all continuous linear functions from
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
to
l_{2}
. For
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, we denote the
k
-th component of the sequence
u(\varphi)
by
\langle u^{k},\varphi\rangle
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

We recall the definition of distributions (generalized functions). The space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
denotes all linear continuous functionals on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Similarly,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
denotes the space of all
l_{2}
-valued continuous linear functions on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. We consider the weak*-topologies on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
. Consequently, the Borel sets generated by these topologies can be used to discuss the measurability of functions whose ranges lie in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

It is easy to observe that both
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
are larger than the class of all tempered distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Additionally, it is obvious that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\not\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\not\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. However, there is an interesting relation that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Another connection between the spaces
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
can be established through the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms. Specifically, the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms can be defined on both
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
in such a way that they can be interchanged. Here are definitions of the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms for elements in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, which follows the conventional method to define these transforms in a weak sense.

Definition 2.2

(Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms of elements in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
)
.

For any
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms of
u
are defined in the following canonical way:

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u],\varphi\rangle:=\langle u,\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\rangle

and

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u],\varphi\rangle:=\langle u,\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\rangle

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Thus for any
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, we have

\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

since it is evident that both
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]
and
\mathcal{F}[\varphi]
are in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
due to well-known properties of the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms of a Schwartz function.
The continuity of
\mathcal{F}[u]
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]
on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
to be distributions is also easily obtained from properties of the Fourier transform, inverse Fourier transform, and the Schwartz functions.
Similarly, for
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
, we define

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle:=\langle u^{k},\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\rangle

and

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle:=\langle u^{k},\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\rangle

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
Simply, by
\mathcal{F}[u]
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]
, we denote the
l_{2}
-valued continuous linear functions on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
so that for any
k\in\mathbf{N}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
the
k
-th terms of
\mathcal{F}[u](\varphi)
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u](\varphi)
are
\langle\mathcal{F}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle
, respectively.

Definition 2.3

(Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms of elements in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
)
.

Let
u
be a distribution on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
i.e.

u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
The Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms of
u
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u],\varphi\rangle:=\langle u,\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\rangle\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

and

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u],\varphi\rangle:=\langle u,\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\rangle\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

Then both
\mathcal{F}[u]
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]
belong to
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Similarly, for
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
, we define

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle:=\langle u^{k},\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\rangle

and

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle:=\langle u^{k},\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\rangle

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
Here,
\mathcal{F}[u]
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]
denote the
l_{2}
-valued continuous linear functions on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
so that for any
k\in\mathbf{N}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
the
k
-th terms of
\mathcal{F}[u](\varphi)
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u](\varphi)
are
\langle\mathcal{F}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]^{k},\varphi\rangle
, respectively.

It can be easily demonstrated that both
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
are homeomorphisms between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. We omit a detailed proof as it follows the classical proof that the Fourier transform is an automorphism on the class of all tempered distributions (
cf.

[
15
, Theorem 2.4]
and references therein).

Theorem 2.4
.

Both Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms are homeomorphisms from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
onto
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
with respect to the weak*-topologies.
The inverse mappings of the homeomorphisms are given by the inverse Fourier transform and the Fourier transform, respectively.

It is well-known that any locally integrable function on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
is a distribution on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Specifically, a locally integrable function with exponential growth at infinity is included in the class of all distributions but not in the class of tempered distributions. Controlling the exponential growth with respect to the frequencies of solutions and data is a major task in achieving our results since this naturally occurs when allowing symbols
\psi
to be sign-changing. This necessity to handle exponential growth is one of the main reasons we need to extend the Fourier transform to include all distributions. Here is a more detailed mathematical explanation of distributions and their realizations as locally integrable functions.

Definition 2.5

(Locally integrable functions and realizations of distributions)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open subset of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
We say that
v
is locally integrable on
\mathcal{U}
if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{K}|v(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K$ of $\mathcal{U}$}.

In particular, it is easy to show that
v
is locally integrable on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}|v(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty),

where
B_{R}
denotes the Euclidean ball whose center is zero and radius is
R
,
i.e.

\displaystyle B_{R}:=\{y\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:|y|<R\}.

We write
v\in L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})
if
v
is locally integrable on
\mathcal{U}
.
Recall that any locally integrable function
v
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
is a distribution on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
due to the identification
v
with the mapping

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}v(x)\overline{\varphi(x)}\mathrm{d}x,

(2.4)

where
\overline{\varphi(x)}
denotes the complex conjugate of
\varphi(x)
.
In this sense,
L_{1,loc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and there is the subspace topology on
L_{1,loc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Additionally, we say that
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
has a realization
v
on
\mathcal{U}
if there exists a locally integrable function
v
on
\mathcal{U}
so that

\displaystyle\langle u,\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}v(x)\overline{\varphi(x)}\mathrm{d}x\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}),

where
\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
denotes the set of all infinitely differentiable functions on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
with compact supports in
\mathcal{U}
.
We simply say that
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is locally integrable on
\mathcal{U}
if it has a realization
v
on
\mathcal{U}
.

Similarly, we use the notation
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U};l_{2})
to denote the space of all
l_{2}
-valued locally integrable functions
v
on
\mathcal{U}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{K}|v(\xi)|_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K$ of $\mathcal{U}$ }.

In particular,
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
becomes a subspace of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
by considering the canonical identification

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\mapsto\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}v^{1}(x)\overline{\varphi(x)}\mathrm{d}x,\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}v^{2}(x)\overline{\varphi(x)}\mathrm{d}x,\cdots\right).

We say that
u=(u^{1},u^{2},\cdots)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
has a realization
v=(v^{1},v^{2},\cdots)
on
\mathcal{U}
if

v\in L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U};l_{2})
and

\displaystyle\langle u^{k},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}v^{k}(x)\overline{\varphi(x)}\mathrm{d}x\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})~\text{and}~\forall k\in\mathbf{N}.

We simply say that
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
is (
l_{2}
-valued) locally integrable on
\mathcal{U}
if it has a realization
v
on
\mathcal{U}
.

Here, we only considered the restrictions of
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
on
\mathcal{U}
rather than defining local distributions on
\mathcal{U}
to circumvent difficulties in combining or extending them.
This seems to be reasonable since our solutions and data are all defined on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
(in a generalized sense).

Furthermore, we temporarily equip
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
with the weak*-topology, which is derived from the subspace topology of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, instead of the strong topology generated by the semi-norms
\|\cdot\|_{L_{1}(B_{R})}
. This choice is because the coarse topology is more suitable for handling the Fourier transform and the inverse Fourier transform due to Theorem
2.4
.
We will revisit
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
with the strong topology when we examine
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued continuous functions later on.

Definition 2.6

(The space of the inverse Fourier transforms of locally integrable functions)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open subset of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Due to Theorem
2.4
,

\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U}):=\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]:u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\cap L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U})\}

is a subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
In other words,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U})
is the space of all the inverse Fourier transforms of distributions on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
which has a realization on
\mathcal{U}
.
In a simpler way, we say that
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U})
has the Fourier transform which is locally integrable on
\mathcal{U}
.
By applying Definition
2.2
and the identification from (
2.4
),
for any
v\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle v,\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}[v],\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi.

Here, the obvious inclusion
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is used
to ensure
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
by extending
\varphi
trivially with zeros outside of
\mathcal{U}
(or simply by the definition of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
).
In particular, it is obvious that for any
v\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U})
,
\mathcal{F}[v]
has a realization on
\mathcal{U}
.
For this case, we say that
v
has a
realizable frequency function
on
\mathcal{U}
.

Similarly, we use the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U};l_{2})
to denote the subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
consisting of all
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]
with
u\in L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U};l_{2})\cap\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.
In particular, for all
v=(v^{1},v^{2},\cdots)\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,loc}(\mathcal{U};l_{2})
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U})
, and
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle v^{k},\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi]\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}[v^{k}],\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{F}[v^{k}](\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi.

Based on these realizations of the elements in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, we are now ready to define the operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
. To define the operator universally for all
t
, we slightly abuse the notation by considering
\psi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
with a function
\psi(\xi)
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
as described below.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
so that it has a realizable frequency function on an open set
\mathcal{U}
covering the support of
\psi
and

\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto\psi(\xi)\mathcal{F}[g](\xi)\in\mathbf{C}
be a locally integrable function on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Here the function
\psi(\xi)\mathcal{F}[g](\xi)
is extended to be complex-valued over the entire
\mathbf{R}^{d}
by assigning it zero outside of
\mathcal{U}
.
Then we define
\psi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)g
as an element in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
so that

\displaystyle\langle\psi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)g,\varphi\rangle:=\langle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\psi\mathcal{F}[g]\right],\varphi\rangle=\langle\psi\mathcal{F}[g],\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\rangle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\psi(\xi)\mathcal{F}[g](\xi)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

(2.5)

It is clear that the open set
\mathcal{U}
is not uniquely determined according to the
g
.
However, it does not affect the value of the last right-hand side of (
2.5
), which implies that our operator
\psi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
is well-defined.
In particular, if
f
is a locally integrable function on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, then

\displaystyle\langle\psi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)(\mathcal{F}^{-1}[f]),\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\psi(\xi)f(\xi)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

due to Theorem
2.4
.

Now it is time to define classes used to treat solutions and data.
We recognize that constructing these necessary classes is inherently complicated as they encompass not only random functions but also various types of linear functions such as
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
, and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.
Although defining all these classes may be tedious, it is a crucial foundational step to ensure the rigor of our theories.
Before specifically defining all spaces, recall that the weak*-topologies are given on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
, and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.
As a result, there are corresponding Borel sets on these spaces, which allows us to consider measurable functions that take values in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
, or
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

Definition 2.8

(
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued random variable)
.

(i)

By
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, we denote the class of all
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued
\mathscr{G}
-measurable random variables
u
such that for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle|\langle u,\varphi\rangle|<\infty\quad(a.s.).

(ii)

\mathbb{L}_{1}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

consisting of elements
u
such that for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle u,\varphi\rangle|\right]<\infty.

Definition 2.9

(
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued processes)
.

Let
\mathscr{H}|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}
be the restriction of
\mathscr{H}
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
, which is defined by the sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}
so that

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}=\{A\cap\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}:A\in\mathscr{H}\},

where

\displaystyle\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}:=\{(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times(0,\infty):0<t\leq\tau(\omega)\}

and

\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}
denotes the restriction of
\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,
i.e.

\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}=\{A\cap\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}:A\in\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\}.

(i)

By
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, we denote the space of
all
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued functions
u
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
such that
for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle|\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle|:=|\langle u(\omega,t),\varphi\rangle|<\infty\quad(a.e.)~(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},

and the mapping
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\mapsto\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle

is
\mathscr{H}|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}
-measurable,
equivalently, the mapping
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle

is
\mathscr{H}
-measurable, where

\displaystyle 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t):=1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(\omega,t):=\begin{cases}&1\quad\text{if $(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}$}\\
&0\quad\text{if $(\omega,t)\notin\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}$}.\end{cases}

Here the trivial extension by zero is used to ensure the term
\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle
is well-defined on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
.
We also introduce an important subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
that possesses certain integrability properties.

(ii)

We use the notation
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

to denote the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all elements
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}|\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle|\mathrm{d}t:=\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}|\langle u(\omega,t),\varphi\rangle|\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad(a.s.).

(iii)

\mathbb{L}_{1,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all elements
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that for all
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}|\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle|\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty,

where
a\wedge b:=\min\{a,b\}
.

(iv)

\mathbb{L}_{1,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{1,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all elements
u\in\mathbb{L}_{1,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}|\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle|\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

Similarly, we can readily define
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued processes.
These processes are typically associated with integrands of Itô integrals, which are well-known in Krylov’s analytic theories for SPDEs.
Therefore, we restrict our attention to predictable processes.

Definition 2.10

(
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued processes)
.

Recall that
\mathcal{P}
is the predictable
\sigma
-algebra.

(i)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
denotes the space of all
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued function
u
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
such that for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\left|\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle\right|_{l_{2}}:=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left|\langle u^{k}(t),\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}:=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left|\langle u^{k}(\omega,t),\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}<\infty\quad(a.e.)~(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}

(2.6)

and
(\omega,t)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle
is
\mathcal{P}
-measurable.

(ii)

\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
denotes
the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
consisting of all
u
such that
for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\left|\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t:=\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left|\langle u(\omega,t,\cdot)^{k},\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad(a.s.).

(iii)

\mathbb{L}_{1,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
denotes the subspace of

\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
consisting of all
u
such that
for all
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

(iv)

\mathbb{L}_{1,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
denotes the subspace of

\mathbb{L}_{1,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
consisting of all
u
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}\left|\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

Remark 2.11
.

The predictable measurability
\mathcal{P}
could be relaxed to progressive measurability. However, we chose predictable measurability for two reasons. First, predictable measurability is sufficient for considering classes of integrands for various stochastic integrals derived from (càdlàg) local martingales. Therefore, we believe that this formulation is more suitable for future research. Second, we adopt many terminologies from Krylov’s theories which utilize predictable
\sigma
-algebras instead of progressive
\sigma
-algebras.

The class of locally integrable functions is a key subset of distributions, as they realize distributions through integral actions, as detailed in Definition
2.5
.
Since
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, there exists the natural
\sigma
-algebra on
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
inherited from the Borel sets on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Alternatively, one can directly construct the Borel sets on
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
using the weak*-topology on
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
This allows for the consideration of measurable functions that take values in
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Additionally, subclasses such as locally integrable function-valued random variables and stochastic processes are intimately connected with Fourier transforms of solutions and data. Below, we provide the notations relevant to these subclasses.
Detailed explanations are omitted because they are straightforward modifications of the previous definitions.

Definition 2.12

(locally integrable function-valued processes)
.

All spaces

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),

and
\mathbb{L}_{1,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)

are defined similarly as in Definitions
2.8
,
2.9
, and
2.10
by considering
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
instead of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

The extended Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms are essential in establishing our primary operators as defined in Definition
2.7
.
Additionally, they act as homeomorphisms between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, as demonstrated in Theorem
2.4
.
This connection allows us to define the classes that manage our solutions and data in a general context.

Definition 2.13

(
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued processes)
.

All spaces

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),

and
\mathbb{L}_{1,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)

are defined similarly as in Definitions
2.8
,
2.9
, and
2.10
by considering
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
instead of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

By Theorem
2.4
, it is obvious that

u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)~\text{if and only if}~\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Similarly,

u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
if and only if

\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
.
All other spaces have the same equivalences.

By Theorem
2.4
again, if
u\in L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, then
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Additionally,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
denotes the class of all
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{F}[u]\in L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
i.e.

\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\iff\mathcal{F}[u]\in L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

In particular, it is evident that for any
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
u
has a realizable frequency function on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u],\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

Similarly,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
is defined as the class of all the inverse Fourier transforms of
u
in
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

We are now ready to define
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued processes, which form essential subclasses of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued processes. Here
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
can be considered as a subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
. These subclasses are crucial for understanding solutions and data characterized by frequencies derived from complex or
l_{2}
-valued stochastic processes.
Additionally, we consider the Borel sets in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
to discuss the measurability of functions that take values in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

Definition 2.14

(
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued processes)
.

All spaces

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),

and

\mathbb{L}_{1,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)

are defined similarly as in Definition
2.12
by considering
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
instead of
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
.
Then it is possible to consider
u
as a complex-valued function defined on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
by putting

\displaystyle u(t,\xi):=u(t)(\xi)

In addition, it is obvious that for each
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
, the mapping

\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto u(t,\xi)

is
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.
However, the joint measurability with respect to
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is generally not guaranteed.

The joint measurability can be restored if
u
has integrability with respect to the product measures.
Specifically, if
u
belongs to a better space
\mathbb{L}_{1,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, then a
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable modification of
u
can be found, leveraging an approximation due to the separability of the space
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Moreover, it is possible to find a jointly measurable modification even without a strong integrability condition. This procedure will be described in detail after introducing some classes of complex-valued (or
l_{2}
-valued) functions.

Definition 2.15

((finite) measurable functions)
.

(i)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the space of all
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable functions on
\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the space of all
\mathscr{H}|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable functions
u
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

(iii)

\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
denotes the space of all
l_{2}
-valued
\mathcal{P}|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable functions
u
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Now we are prepared to identify our complex-valued (or
l_{2}
-valued) classes as subclasses of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued (or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued) processes.
Here is the core idea.
Let
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and assume that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}|u(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

(2.7)

Then for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, the mapping

\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}u(\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi

is in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Additionally, for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, the mapping

\omega\in\Omega\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}u(\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi:=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}u(\omega,\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi

is
\mathscr{G}
-measurable by Fubini’s theorem since the probability space is complete.
Thus, the mapping

\omega\in\Omega\mapsto\left(\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}u(\omega,\xi)\varphi(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

is a
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued
\mathscr{G}
-measurable function on
\Omega
since the weak*-topology and its Borel sets are given on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
More precisely,
u
is a
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued
\mathscr{G}
-measurable function on
\Omega
.
Therefore
u
becomes an element in
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
if (
2.7
) holds.
Moreover, one can consider the Fourier transform or the inverse Fourier transform of
u
(for each fixed
\omega
) and specifically,

\mathcal{F}^{-1}[u]\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Next, let
v\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
.
Then for each
\omega\in\Omega
, the mapping
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)\in\mathbf{C}
becomes a complex-valued
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function defined
(a.e.)
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Thus, we can regard
\mathcal{F}[v](\xi):=\mathcal{F}[v](\omega,\xi):=\mathcal{F}[v(\omega)](\xi)
as a complex-valued function defined on
\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that for each
\omega\in\Omega
, the mapping
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)
is
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.
Furthermore, for any
R\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.).

However, there is no guarantee that

(\omega,\xi)\in\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathcal{F}[v](\omega,\xi)
is
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.
Without joint measurability, it is impossible to apply the Fubini theorem.
Thus, it needs to introduce a subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
with joint measurability.

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{F}[v]
has better integrability so that
\mathbb{E}[\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}|\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi]<\infty
, then one can easily find a
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable modification of
\mathcal{F}[u]
based on an approximation of nice functions. Here, the nice functions consist of finite sums of linear combinations of
(X\otimes f)(\omega,x):=X(\omega)f(x)
, where
X\in L_{1}(\Omega,\mathscr{G})
and
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Furthermore, it is also possible to find a joint-measurable modification of
\mathcal{F}[v]

without integrability by using the approximation
1_{|\xi|<n}\cdot\left(-n\vee\mathcal{F}[v](\xi)\wedge n\right)
to
\mathcal{F}[v]
as
n\to\infty
.

Nonetheless, we prefer to consider classes of jointly measurable functions to avoid the complexity of finding modifications.
Thus we provide more precise definitions of subspaces to address joint measurability.

Definition 2.16
.

(i)

\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable complex-valued functions
u
on
\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}|u(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

(ii)

\mathbb{L}_{1,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable complex-valued functions
u
on
\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|u(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

(iii)

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that the mapping

\displaystyle(\omega,\xi)\in\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathcal{F}[u](\xi):=\mathcal{F}[u](\omega,\xi):=\mathcal{F}[u(\omega)](\xi)\in\mathbf{C}

is
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

(iv)

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

If we consider a function defined on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, then all the measurability issues become much more complicated.
Recall that
\mathscr{H}
is a sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
and

\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the class of all
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued
\mathscr{H}|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}
-measurable functions defined in

\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
.
Let
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and suppose that for each
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,
u(\omega,t)
has a realization on
\mathcal{U}
,
i.e.
for any compact subset
K\subset\mathcal{U}
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}|u(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi:=\int_{K}|u(\omega,t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi:=\int_{K}|u(\omega,t)(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.e.)~(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}.

(2.8)

By
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})\right)
, we denote the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
so that (
2.8
) holds.

In particular,
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
if
\mathcal{U}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
u
can be identified with a complex-valued function defined on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
by putting

\displaystyle u(t,\xi):=u(\omega,t,\xi):=u(\omega,t)(\xi).

However, unless
\mathcal{U}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
, then this identification does not work properly for
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})\right)

with a complex-valued function defined on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}
according to the trivial extension

u(t,\xi)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}}(t,\xi):=u(\omega,t,\xi)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}}(\omega,t,\xi):=u(\omega,t)(\xi)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}}(\omega,t,\xi)

since it is not guaranteed to be an element in
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
.

In particular, this identification is easily possible for special
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})\right)
which has a compact support with respect to
\xi
as a distribution uniformly for all
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
by considering the canonical extension.
Additionally, for any
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})\right)
, it is obvious that for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,
the mapping

\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto u(t,\xi)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}}(t,\xi)\in\mathbf{C}

is
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.

However, (
2.8
) does not generally ensure the joint measurability of
u(\omega,t,\xi)
.
Thus we consider a subclasses of
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
with enhanced measurability.
By

\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

we denote the class consisting of
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that (
2.8
) holds with
\mathcal{U}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
and the mapping

(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)u(t,\xi):=1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(\omega,t)u(\omega,t,\xi)

is
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.
Moreover, we need to define a subclass of
