\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of all elements having (spatial) realizable frequency functions on open sets in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Formally, it is given by

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}u:u(\omega,t,\cdot)\in L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})\},

where
\mathcal{U}
is an open set in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
However, it is difficult to define it rigorously since
all we know is that for each
(\omega,t)
,
u(\omega,t,x)
becomes a complex-valued function only on
\mathcal{U}
.
Thus we redefine
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})
as the subspace of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
consisting of
u
having a realization on
\mathcal{U}
.
Then
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathcal{U})\right)
implies that
u
is a locally integrable function on
\mathcal{U}
but also a distribution on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
for each
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
due to the definition of the class.
We provide rigorous definitions below with the additional consideration that
\mathcal{U}
varies depending on the time variable.

Definition 2.17
.

We consider a family of open subsets of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
indexed by
(0,\infty)
and denoted by
\{\mathcal{U}_{t}\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}:t\in(0,\infty)\}
.
We put

\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}:=\left\{(\omega,t,\xi)\in\Omega\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}:0<t\leq\tau(\omega)~\text{and}~\xi\in\mathcal{U}_{t}\right\}.

Here, we employ the notation
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}
somewhat loosely since
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is determined by each
t\in(0,\tau(\omega)]
.
In other words, for each
\omega
,
(0,\tau(\omega)]\times\mathcal{U}_{t}:=\{(t,\xi)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}:t\in(0,\infty)~\text{and}~\xi\in\mathcal{U}_{t}\}
is actually a subset of
(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
but it is not a strict Cartesian product.
More precisely,

\displaystyle(\omega,t,\xi)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}\quad\iff\quad(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}~\text{and}~\xi\in\mathcal{U}_{t}\quad\iff\omega\in\Omega,~t\in(0,\tau(\omega)],~\text{and}~\xi\in\mathcal{U}_{t}.

(i)

For any
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
,
we write
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

if there exists a complex-valued function
f(\omega,t,\xi)
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}

such that
the mapping
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)f(\omega,t,\xi)
is
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable, and for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\left|f(\omega,t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K$ of $\mathcal{U}_{t}$},

and

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)],\varphi\rangle:=\langle\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)],\varphi\rangle:=\langle\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t)],\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{U}_{t}}f(\omega,t,\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t}).

In particular, we write
\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)=f(\omega,t,\xi)
by identifying two mappings

(\omega,t)\mapsto\left(\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t})\mapsto\langle\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)],\varphi\rangle\right)

and

(\omega,t)\mapsto\left(\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t})\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}f(\omega,t,\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\right).

Thus for any
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, the Fourier transform of
u
(with respect to the space variable) is a function defined on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}
so that the mapping

(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)

is
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable, and for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathcal{U}_{t}$}.

(2.9)

Here (
2.9
) means that there exists a subset
\mathcal{T}\subset\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
so that

\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\setminus\mathcal{T}
is a null set and for each
(\omega,t)\in\mathcal{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{K}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathcal{U}_{t}$}.

(ii)

We use the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
to denote the subspace consisting of all
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{K}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K$ of $\mathbf{R}^{d}$},

which means that there exists a
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
so that
P(\Omega^{\prime})=1
and for all
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
and compact subset
K
of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}\int_{K}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

(iii)

We use the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
to denote the subspace consisting of all
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that for all
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\int_{K}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K$ of $\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

(iv)

We use the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
to denote the subspace consisting of all
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{K}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K$ of $\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

Here,
K
does not vary with respect to the time variable in (ii), (iii), and (iv).

All the spaces mentioned above appear quite complex due to the presence of the family of open subsets
\mathcal{U}_{t}
.
General definitions involving
\mathcal{U}_{t}
are provided to demonstrate that complicate identifications can work for general domains to some extent, potentially aiding future research. However, we usually set
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
uniformly for all
t
.
In fact, in this paper, we only consider the case where
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
uniformly for all
t
.
This simplifies all definitions considerably. Moreover, it is clear that “for all compact subsets
K
of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
” can be replaced by “for all
B_{R}:=\{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:|\xi|<R\}
with
R\in(0,\infty)
” due to the Heine-Borel theorem.

Next, we extend the spaces defined in Definition
2.17
by incorporating non-negative weights. In particular, we aim to permit weights to be zero on sets that have positive measures. Under these conditions, elements cease to be unique as complex-valued functions because they can assume any value in regions where the weights are zero. We resolve this issue by considering the weighted spaces as subspaces of the distribution-valued spaces previously introduced.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
W
be a non-negative
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function on
\Omega\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, and
\{\mathcal{U}_{t}:t\in(0,\infty)\}
be a family of open subsets of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
so that
\supp W(t,\cdot)\subset\mathcal{U}_{t}
with probability one, where

\displaystyle\supp W(t,\cdot):=\overline{\{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:W(t,\xi):=W(\omega,t,\xi)\neq 0\}}

and
\overline{\{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:W(t,\xi)\neq 0\}}
denotes the closure of the set
\{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}:W(t,\xi)\neq 0\}
with respect to the topology generated by the Euclidean norm on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

(i)

For any
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
,
we write
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

if there exists a complex-valued function
f(\omega,t,\xi)
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}
such that
for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t)],\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{U}_{t}}f(\omega,t,\xi)\varphi(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t}),

(2.10)

the mapping
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)f(\omega,t,\xi)
is
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable, and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)\left|f(\omega,t,\xi)\right|W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

(2.11)

In particular, we write
\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)=\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)=f(\omega,t,\xi)
by identifying two mappings

(\omega,t)\mapsto\left(\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t})\mapsto\langle\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t)],\varphi\rangle\right)

and

(\omega,t)\mapsto\left(\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t})\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}f(\omega,t,\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\right).

In other words, for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
, the Fourier transform of
u
,

\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)]:=\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t)]
, has a realization on
\mathcal{U}_{t}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|W(\omega,t,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

(2.12)

Thus for any

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right),

the Fourier transform of
u
(with respect to the space variable) is a function defined on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}
such that the mapping
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)
is
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable, and (
2.12
) holds.
In other words, any
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)
(for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
) has a realizable frequency function on
\mathcal{U}_{t}
.

(ii)

We use

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

to denote the subspace of

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

consisting of all elements

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

so that for each
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

Additionally,

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

denotes the subspace of

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

consisting of all elements whose expectation is finite,
i.e.

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

if and only if

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

Remark 2.19
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)
.
Then obviously,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty).

It might seem that
\mathcal{F}[u]
could increase very rapidly within a bounded domain if
W
strongly diminishes there.
However, the extent of the blow-up is restricted and cannot disrupt local integrability because
u
must satisfy the additional condition that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{U}_{t}}\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\overline{\varphi(\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\right|<\infty\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{U}_{t}).

Due to this condition, it is also obvious that

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, which clearly implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Remark 2.20
.

It appears that all function spaces in Definition
2.18
depend on the choice of a family of open subsets

\{\mathcal{U}_{t}\}
.
However, the (local) weighted
L_{1}
-norms in (
2.11
) are unaffected by this choice because of the condition

\supp W(t,\cdot)\subset\mathcal{U}_{t}
with probability one.
Furthermore, there is always the trivial option of setting
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
for all
t
.
This trivial case is considered throughout the paper.
In other words, local distributions are not a primary focus since our equation is solved in the entire space with respect to the spatial variable. Nonetheless, each
\mathcal{U}_{t}
can be chosen to be as close to

\supp W(t,\cdot)
as desired, provided the support is uniformly given for the sample points
\omega\in\Omega
.

Remark 2.21
.

We do consider random weights in Definition
2.18
since our symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
may involve randomness, which leads to the emergence of certain random weighted spaces related to the symbol. We had restricted the definition of weighted spaces to suit our specific needs. Additionally, these classes are used to characterize our solutions according to the influence of the symbols. However, we do not address general weighted estimates with randomness, which, to the best of our knowledge, remains an unexplored area in theories of stochastic partial differential equations. Nonetheless, our approach enables the definitions of more general weighted spaces, such as

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{p,q,r,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi;l_{2}\right)
for all
p,q,r\in[1,\infty)
, although these are not utilized in the paper.

Remark 2.22
.

Recall that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)
is a subspace of

\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

However, it is not a metric space generated by the weighted
L_{1}
-norms in (
2.11
) if the weight
W(t,\xi)=0
on a set having a positive measure.
In other words, there may exist two different
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
in
\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\omega,t,\xi)\left|\mathcal{F}[u_{1}(\omega,t,\xi)]-\mathcal{F}[u_{2}(\omega,t,\xi)]\right|W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right]=0\quad\forall R\in(0,\infty)

(2.13)

if the weight
W
is degenerate on a set with a positive measure.
Therefore the space

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

itself does not seem to be appropriate for discussing the uniqueness of a solution
based solely on estimates like (
2.13
).

Next, we examine
l_{2}
-valued correspondents, focusing solely on the predictable
\sigma
-algebra. Elements in these spaces act as stochastic inhomogeneous data and serve as integrands for Itô stochastic integrals, as previously mentioned.
Additionally, we consider only unweighted spaces, since we do not address weighted stochastic inhomogeneous data. It should be noted that some classes with different orders of integration are included to apply the recently developed stochastic Fubini theorems, which operate under weaker assumptions and are introduced in Section
5
.
For simplicity, the trivial case
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\mathbf{R}^{d}
for all
t
is considered in the following definition and some notational details from the previous definitions are omitted.

Definition 2.23
.

(i)

For any
u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
,
we write

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

if the mapping
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(\omega,t)\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)
is a
l_{2}
-valued
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function and for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

(ii)

We use the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
to denote the subspace consisting of all
u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{K}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

(iii)

The notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
describes the subspace consisting of all

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

such that for each
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{K}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

(iv)

Finally,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
denotes the subspace consisting of all

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{K}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

Remark 2.24
.

The superscript in the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

indicates the order of the integration.
Specifically,

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

if and only if
for each
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{K}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[u(\omega,t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

Similarly, the notation

u\in\mathbb{L}^{\omega,x,t}_{1,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

implies that for each
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{K}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau(\omega)\wedge T}\left|u(\omega,t,x)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x\right]<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

If no superscript is provided, the integration order follows the sequence of the product.
For instance, if

u\in\mathbb{L}_{1,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\tau}\left(\int_{K}|u(t,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad\text{for all compact subsets $K\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}$}.

Additionally, all complex-valued function spaces are defined without superscripts, which means that the order of the integrations is always in the sequence of the random variable
\omega
, time variable
t
, and space variable
x
.

Typically, it is anticipated that solutions to SPDEs driven by space-time white noise exhibit continuous paths as all paths of Brownian motions are continuous. However, our operator is quite irregular, meaning its symbol lacks regularity. Thus it is difficult to expect that the paths of our solutions will remain continuous. Nonetheless, we can still assert a certain form of path continuity in a weak sense using test functions. Specifically, the continuity of paths of solutions to (
2.2
) can be maintained as a
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued functions. We establish these concepts by first defining distribution-valued continuous processes.

Definition 2.25

(Spaces of continuous paths)
.

We say that a function
u:\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is a
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued
\mathscr{H}
-measurable process
if for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, (noting that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is equipped with the the weak*-topology)

\displaystyle(\omega,t)\mapsto 1_{\text{$[$\kern-1.19579pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle:=1_{\text{$[$\kern-1.19579pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(\omega,t)\langle u(\omega,t),\varphi\rangle

is
\mathscr{H}
-measurable, where

\displaystyle\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}=\{(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty):0\leq t\leq\tau(\omega)\}

and

\displaystyle 1_{\text{$[$\kern-1.19579pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(\omega,t):=\begin{cases}&1\quad\text{if}~(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\\
&0\quad\text{if}~(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)\setminus\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}.\end{cases}

By
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, we denote the space of all
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued
\mathscr{H}
-measurable processes such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, the paths

t\mapsto\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle

are continuous on
[0,\tau]
with probability one,
i.e.
there exists a
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
so that
P(\Omega^{\prime})=1
and for all
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,
the mapping

\displaystyle t\in[0,\tau(\omega)]\mapsto\langle u(\omega,t),\varphi\rangle\in\mathbf{C}

is continuous.

The notation
\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of elements with finite expectations,
i.e.

u\in\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
if and only if

u\in\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\langle u(t),\varphi\rangle\right|\right]<\infty\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

The spaces
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and
\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

are defined in a similar way by substituting
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
for
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Then, it is obvious that

u\in\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

if and only if

\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
.

Finally, we also define important subspaces

\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),~\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

and
\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
by considering the subspaces
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
instead of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, respectively.

Remark 2.26
.

There are typically two ways to define topologies on
L_{1,loc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
The first method is to equip
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
with the subspace topology of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
so called the weak*-topology, which we denote by
\mathscr{T}_{w}
. This is the topology we have used on
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
up to this point.
The second method involves using the strong topology generated by the local semi-norms

\|u\|_{L_{1}(B_{R})}=\int_{B_{R}}|u(x)|\mathrm{d}x\quad R\in(0,\infty)

and use the notation
\mathscr{T}_{s}
to denote this topology.
Then it is obvious that
\mathscr{T}_{w}\subset\mathscr{T}_{s}
.
Additionally, the space

\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,loc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

can be equipped with either of these topologies.
By
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{weak}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, we denote the space
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,loc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
with topologies
\mathscr{T}_{w}
and
\mathscr{T}_{s}
, respectively.
Here we have not defined
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
rigorously yet, but it can be understood as space of all continuous functions from
[0,\tau]
to
L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
with probability one.

Now first consider the space
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{weak}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
.
Then this topology has an advantage because it can be easily translated to the topology on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
by taking the inverse Fourier transform, due to the homeomorphism in Theorem
2.4
.
Additionally, a coarser topology is preferable when it suffices for constructing results.
In fact, the weak*-topology is adequate for developing our theories.

However, we can naturally relate the semi-norms to the space
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
to measure sizes of elements.
Additionally, there is a trivial continuous embedding that

\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{weak}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

according to the identity mapping
x\in L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\mapsto x\in L^{weak}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Thus, the space

\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

is preferred for considering a solution because it inherently provides more information and is more optimized.

Lastly, any
u\in\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
can be regarded as a complex-valued function defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
so that for any
R\in(0,\infty)

\displaystyle t\in[0,\tau]\mapsto\int_{B_{R}}|u(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x

is continuous and thus

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\int_{B_{R}}|u(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty

(2.14)

with probability one by putting
u(\omega,t,x)=u(\omega,t)(x)
.
Conversely, for a nice complex-valued function
u(\omega,t,x)
defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
can be recognized as an element of

\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
by considering the mappings

\displaystyle(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\mapsto\int_{B_{R}}|u(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x\quad R\in(0,\infty)

so that

\displaystyle t\in[0,\tau]\mapsto\int_{B_{R}}|u(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x

is continuous with probability one and the condition

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}|u(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.).

(2.15)

holds.
Therefore, one can consider a subspace of
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L^{strong}_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of complex-valued joint measurable functions defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
without losing much information.
Here (
2.15
) becomes stronger than (
2.14
) to derive the
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued continuity from the continuity of
t\mapsto u(\omega,t,x)
for almost every
\omega
and
x
.
The detail for the definition is as follows.

Definition 2.27

(Spaces of realizable continuous paths)
.

Let
u
be a (complex-valued)
\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

(i)

We write
u\in\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
if for any
R
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}|u(t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.),

and the mapping
t\in[0,\tau]\mapsto u(t,x)
is continuous for almost every
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
with probability one,
i.e.
there exists a
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
such that
P(\Omega^{\prime})=1
, for any
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
and
R\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau(\omega)]}|u(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty

and
t\in[0,\tau(\omega)]\mapsto u(\omega,t,x)
is continuous for almost every
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace consisting of
u
such that

u\in\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and for any
R
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}|u(t,x)|\mathrm{d}x\right]<\infty.

Remark 2.28
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Then by applying the dominate convergence theorem,

t\in[0,\tau]\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}u(t,x)\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x
is continuous with probability one.
Additionally, it is obvious that for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
, the function
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto u(t,x)
is in
L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

(2.16)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Due to (
2.16
), one can consider the inverse Fourier transform of
u
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}u\in\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

More precisely, for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[u(\omega,t,\cdot)\right]\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

Therefore, we can consider the spaces consisting of the inverse Fourier transforms of

u\in\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Definition 2.29

(Spaces of inverse Fourier transforms of realizable continuous paths)
.

(i)

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of
u
such that

\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

(ii)

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
denotes the subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}L_{1,\ell oc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
consisting of
u
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u]\in\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Remark 2.30
.

Let

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\leq\tau\cdot\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\quad(a.s.).

Thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,loc}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Remark 2.31
.

We treat all the spaces mentioned above as the quotient spaces generated by the equivalence relation which is “equal almost everywhere”.
For instance, a
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
-measurable function defined
v
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
is in
\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)
if there exists a
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})
-valued
\mathcal{P}
-measurable functions
u
on
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
such that
for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\langle v(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle=\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle<\infty\quad(a.e.)~(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},

(2.17)

where
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the completion of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{P}
with respect to the measure
dP\times dt
.
Note that (
2.17
) holds
(a.e.)
for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and it is sufficient to identify
u
and
v
since the space
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is separable.
In particular, if for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\langle v(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle
is a modification
\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle

(so sufficiently two complex-valued processes
\langle v(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle
and
\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle
are indistinguishable),
then we identify
v
and
u
.

Definition 2.32

(Temporal local spaces)
.

We define temporal local spaces based on the typical cutoffs of stopping times.
For instance, we write

\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

if

\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\wedge T\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),W(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

Similarly,

\displaystyle u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

if

\displaystyle u\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\wedge T\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

All other temporal local spaces can be defined in a similar manner.
Some of these temporal local spaces have been already mentioned in Definition
2.17
and Definition
2.23
.
It is easy to verify that these new temporal local spaces are equivalent to the previously defined ones.
Moreover, some spaces remain unchanged under temporal localization.
For example, it is straightforward to confirm that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{0,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)=\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

(2.18)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{loc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)=\mathbb{C}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{H};\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

(2.19)

since
\tau
is a finite stopping time.

It is clear that a temporally localized space is larger than the original one.
Additionally, there are many interesting relationships between them. Specifically, the temporal local property does not affect processes without finite expectations, as our fixed stopping time
\tau
is finite. Simple examples are provided in (
2.18
) and (
2.19
). We present a straightforward proposition to list some of these properties, which are not as obvious as those in (
2.18
) and (
2.19
). Nonetheless, the proof is not difficult, so we omit the details.
These properties help justify the appropriateness of our data classes in the main theorems in the next section.

Proposition 2.33
.

(i)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

(ii)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

As previously noted, all the properties mentioned above are easily derived from the fact that
\tau
is a finite stopping time.
A similar detail for the proof can be observed in Lemma
8.5
later.
However, if we consider a space consisting of elements with finite expectations, then a temporally localized space becomes strictly larger than the original one.
This is because it is easy to find a stochastic process
X_{t}
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}|X_{t}|dt\right]=\infty

but

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}|X_{t}|dt\right]<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty)

unless
\tau
is bounded.

3.
Main results

In this section, we present our main results. We discovered that our operators
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
can be defined without regularity conditions as described in Definition
2.7
. Our next step is to determine the conditions on the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
to ensure that (
2.2
) is well-posed in a certain sense.

This point has been mentioned several times, but we repeat it for emphasis: the symbol
\psi
can be random, sign-changing, and irregular, yet the solvability of (
2.2
) remains preserved.
In essence, our primary assumptions impose only integrability conditions on the symbol
\psi
.

Firstly, we require local boundedness of the symbol uniformly with respect to the sample points
\omega
.
However, it is sufficient to assume local boundedness only on the real part of the symbol.

Assumption 3.1

(Local boundedness on the real part of the symbol)
.

For all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\esssup_{(t,\xi)\in(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right]<\infty.

(3.1)

We also introduce some constants related to Assumption
3.1
, which appear in our main estimates of solutions.
Put

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}=\esssup_{(t,\xi)\in(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)\right].

Then it is obvious that (
3.1
) holds if and only if

C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}<\infty.

Note that our requirement for the local boundedness pertains only to the space variable
\xi
.
In other words,
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]
can be unbounded with respect to the time variable
t
as long as it does not ruin the integrability.

Next, we impose an additional condition on
\psi(t,\xi)
to ensure a control over the product
|\psi(t,\xi)|
and
\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)
.
This relationship is crucial for defining
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u
with a candidate
u
for a solution as per Definition
2.7
and is necessary for applying the fundamental theorem of calculus in the proofs of the main theorems.

Assumption 3.2
.

For all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle C^{\sup|\psi|}_{R,T}:=\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left(\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}|\psi(t,\xi)|\right)\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

(3.2)

In particular, Assumption
3.2
implies that for almost every
(\omega,\xi)\in\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty)

(3.3)

since the term
\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]
is always greater than or equal to one.
Note that
C^{\sup|\psi|}_{R,T}
could be small if the value of
|\psi(t,\xi)|
diminishes.
In other words, we could not generally determine if
C^{|\psi|}_{R,T}\geq 1
.

Remark 3.3
.

All constants and conditions can be simplified if we remove the randomness on
\psi
.
Assume that the symbol
\psi
is non-random (or deterministic),
i.e.

\displaystyle\psi(\omega_{1},t,\xi)=\psi(\omega_{2},t,\xi)\quad\forall\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega.

Then (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) become

\displaystyle\esssup_{(t,\xi)\in(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right]=\esssup_{(t,\xi)\in(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right]<\infty

and

\displaystyle\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}|\psi(t,\xi)|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle=\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left(\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}|\psi(t,\xi)|\right)\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty,

respectively.
Moreover, these conditions can be replaced by the weaker conditions

\displaystyle\esssup_{(t,\xi)\in(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|<\infty

and

\displaystyle\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}|\psi(t,\xi)|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\exp\left(\left|\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

This will be detailed in Section
6
and Section
8
.

Remark 3.4
.

By the fundamental theorem of calculus,

\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)=1+\int_{0}^{t}|\Re[\psi(s,\xi)]|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{s}\left|\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\right|\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, there is the relation between the constants
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}
and
C^{\sup|\psi|}_{R,T}
such that

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}\leq 1+C^{\sup|\psi|}_{R,T}.

Remark 3.5
.

\psi(t,\xi)
does not have to be defined at
t=0
as can be observed in the main equation (
2.2
).
However, our solution
u(t,x)
satisfies
u(0,x)=u_{0}
, which implies the solution
u
is defined at
t=0
in a certain sense.
Furthermore, the measurability of
u
definitely depends on the measurability of the symbol
\psi
.
Thus, to explain this measurability relation, one must consider restrictions or extensions of the
\sigma
-algebras related to both
u
and
\psi
.
Instead, we choose to make
\psi
defined on the whole
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
to avoid all these complications.

Therefore, the value
\psi(0,\xi)
can be arbitrary and it is provided only to simplify the expression of measurability relations which will be detailed in the proofs of our main theorems.
Specifically, all estimates with
\psi
do not depend on
\psi(0,\xi)
at all.
This is why the main assumptions regarding
\psi
do not involve the values of
\psi(0,\xi)
.

In addition to removing regularity conditions and allowing randomness on
\psi
, another novel aspect of our assumptions on
\psi
is that
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]
is permitted to be sign-changing.
In other words, the range of
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]
can include both positive and negative values.
It is crucial to maintain the sign of
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]
to be negative in order to ensure even simpler equations with pseudo-differential operators than (
2.2
) are well-posed in Sobolev’s spaces.
This finding is supported by the results in
[
9
,
11
,
13
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
36
]
.
All the results rely on the condition called “an ellipticity condition” that there exist positive constants
\kappa
and
\gamma
so that

\displaystyle\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]\leq-\kappa|\xi|^{\gamma},

where
\gamma
is understood as an order of the operator
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
.
However, this ellipticity condition does not need to be met under our main assumptions, as we might consider analytically weak solutions rather than strong solutions in Sobolev spaces.
In particular, it is impossible to assign a specific order to our operators, which partially demonstrates their generality.

It is surprising that uniqueness holds in our framework, given that classical theories for the differential equations with constant coefficients on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
suggest that uniqueness cannot be maintained without ellipticity, as mentioned in the introduction.

The relationship between the sign and ellipticity of symbols becomes very clear when examining the second-order case as a concrete example.
Here even the existence of a weak solution cannot be obtained from classical theories (by considering an equation in
\mathbf{R}^{d+1}
) since our coefficients in the following example vary with respect to the time variable.

Example 3.6

(Second-order operators with time-dependent coefficients)
.

For
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, let
a^{ij}(t)
be complex-valued functions such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|a^{ij}(t)|\mathrm{d}t<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

(3.4)

Put

\displaystyle\psi(t,\xi)=-a^{ij}(t)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}.

Then it is easy to verify that
\psi
satisfies Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
.
Additionally, it is obvious that

\displaystyle\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)\varphi=a^{ij}(t)\varphi_{x^{i}x^{j}}

for a nice function
\varphi
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
due to some basic properties of the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms.

We typically say that the coefficients
a^{ij}(t)
satisfy an (weak) ellipticity condition if

\displaystyle a^{ij}(t)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\geq 0\quad\forall t\in(0,\infty).

(3.5)

In particular, the coefficients
a^{ij}(t)
are said to be
degenerate
if
a^{ij}(t)=0
for some
t
.
Recently, there were researches showing some strong
L_{p}
-estimates are still possible even though the coefficients
a^{ij}(t)
are degenerate (
[
40
,
41
,
38
]
).
Our weak solutions encompass all solutions from these results for degenerate second-order equations.

Moreover,
we say that the coefficients
a^{ij}(t)
satisfy a uniform ellipticity condition if there exists a positive constant
\kappa
so that

\displaystyle a^{ij}(t)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\geq\kappa|\xi|^{2}\quad\forall t\in(0,\infty).

(3.6)

Obviously, (
3.5
) implies that the symbol
\psi
is always non-positive.
Especially, the condition in (
3.6
) makes the symbol
\psi
strictly negative.

Note that even the condition in (
3.5
) is not required to satisfy our main assumptions.
The local integrability from (
3.4
) is sufficient to show that
\psi
satisfies Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
.
In conclusion, our operators
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
include second-order operators even without weak ellipticity conditions.

Next, we present another intriguing example that satisfies Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
, involving sign-changing properties and fractional Laplacian operators.

The fractional Laplacian operators usually denoted by
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
have been fascinating subjects of study in mathematics.
Typically, the restriction
\alpha\in(0,2]
is imposed and then the operator
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
becomes a generator of a Lévy process. For this special cases,
\alpha\in(0,2]
, many interesting properties of the operators and their generalizations can be derived from theories of Markov processes (
cf.

[
5
,
6
,
7
,
48
]
).
Additionally, numerous analytic methods exist to study these operators (
cf.

[
3
,
21
,
49
,
62
]
).
There are also tons of researches addressing these operators in equations and can be found, for instance, in
[
10
,
14
,
12
,
18
,
22
,
23
,
39
]
.
Even for the range
\alpha\in(2,\infty)
, some analytic methods are still available to find out the properties of fractional Laplacian operators and the solvability of related equations.
However, there is a lack of results considering fractional Laplacian operators with complex exponents despite the existence of natural analytic continuations.

To the best of our knowledge, our theory is the first to tackle the solvability of stochastic partial differential equations involving fractional Laplacian operators with complex exponents. Furthermore, we have not found any existing PDE results that address equations with fractional Laplacian operators featuring complex exponents.
However, we found a numerical simulation result for this operator as reported by
[
1
]
.
It is unexpectedly revealed that the fractional Laplacian operator with a complex exponent is not only a mathematical generalization but also has scientific significance, as shown in
[
1
]
and the references therein.

Example 3.7

(Fractional Laplacian operators with complex exponents)
.

Let
\alpha=\Re[\alpha]+i\Im[\alpha]
be a complex number and put

\displaystyle\psi(t,\xi)=-|\xi|^{\alpha}.

Then for any nonzero
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\psi(t,\xi)=-\exp\left(\alpha\log|\xi|\right)

\displaystyle=-\exp\left(\Re[\alpha]\log|\xi|+\mathrm{i}\Im[\alpha]\log|\xi|\right)

\displaystyle=-\exp\left(\Re[\alpha]\log|\xi|\right)\cdot\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\Im[\alpha]\log|\xi|\right)

\displaystyle=-|\xi|^{\Re[\alpha]}\left(\cos(\Im[\alpha]\log|\xi|)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\Im[\alpha]\log|\xi|)\right).

For this symbol
\psi
, we adopt the simpler notation

\displaystyle\Delta^{\alpha/2}u(t,x):=\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x)

and this operator is known as the
fractional Laplacian operator
.
Assume that
\Re[\alpha]\geq 0
, then it is easy to check that
\psi
satisfies Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
.

Now we turn our attention to an important inequality in probability theories.

Let
g
be an
l_{2}
-valued square integrable predictable process.
Then for all
p\in(0,\infty)
and
T\in(0,\infty)
, there exist positive constants
c_{p}
and
C_{p}
(depending only on
p
) such that

\displaystyle c_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}|g(s)|^{2p}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/(2p)}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int_{0}^{t}g^{k}(s)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\right|^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}|g(s)|^{2p}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/(2p)}\right].

(3.7)

This inequality is well-known as the BDG (Burkholder-Davis-Gundy) inequality.
A proof can be found, for instance, in
[
2
,
32
,
61
]
.
Especially, the constant
C_{1}
plays an important role in our main theorem.

Definition 3.8

(BDG constants)
.

We call
C_{1}
in (
3.7
)
the BDG constant
and use the special notation
C_{BDG}
instead of
C_{1}
.
The exact value of the smallest
C_{BDG}
is not known to the best of our knowledge.
However, taking
p=1
and
g=(1,0,0,\ldots)
in (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|B^{1}_{t}|\right]\leq C_{BDG}T^{1/2}.

Additionally, by Bachelier’s theorem (
cf
.
[
33
, Theorem 2.2.3]
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|B^{1}_{t}|\right]=2\int_{0}^{\infty}x\exp(-x^{2})\mathrm{d}x\cdot T^{1/2}=T^{1/2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle C_{BDG}\geq 1.

(3.8)

Additionally, it is well-known that
C_{BDG}\leq 3
(
cf
.
[
2
]
and
[
32
,
61
, Section 4 in Chapter IV]
).

This constant is considered to try to provide explicit constants (at least with respect to the constants appearing in the BDG inequality and assumptions) in our main theorem.

Before stating the definition of our solution to (
2.2
), we recall an important property of the space-time white noise
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
so that

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}=\eta_{k}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{k},

where
\{\eta_{k}:k\in\mathbf{N}\}
is the orthonomal basis of
L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
from (
2.3
).
This relation is easily derived since
L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-cylindrical Brownian motions can be constructed from a sequence of independent one-dimensional Brownian motions.
Thus recognizing a SPDE driven by space-time white noise as an equation driven by a sequence of independent one-dimensional white noises has been a common approach in studying the properties of their solutions. For example, see
[
8
,
27
,
31
,
45
,
46
,
47
]
.
In this paper, we also employ this method to provide a precise mathematical meaning to solutions of SPDEs in the form of (
2.2
). This will be detailed in the next section.
For now, we provide the exact definition of our solution.

Definition 3.9

(Fourier-space weak solution to (
2.2
))
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
,

\displaystyle f\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty));\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),\quad h\in\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

and

u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}\left([0,\infty)\right);\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Then we say that
u
is a
Fourier-space weak solution
to equation (
2.2
) if for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u_{0},\varphi\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\psi(s,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle f(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle h(s,\cdot)\eta^{k}(\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.e.)\quad(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}.

(3.9)

Remark 3.10
.

The stochastic term

\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle h(s,\cdot)\eta^{k}(\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

cannot be defined based on a canonical multiplication of a test function.
Specifically, the condition

h\in\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

is insufficient to render the term

\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle h(s,\cdot),\eta^{k}\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

well-defined since
\eta^{k}\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
but
\mathcal{F}[\eta^{k}\varphi]
generally does not have a compact support.
Thus we need to consider a subclass of
\mathbb{L}_{0,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, which ensures that the stochastic term in (
3.9
) is well-defined.
In particular, we will show that the stochastic term

\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle h(s,\cdot)\eta^{k}(\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}:=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left\langle h(s,\cdot)\eta^{k}(\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is well-defined if

h\in\mathbb{L}_{0,2,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

in Corollary
11.2
.

Finally, here is our main result.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
,
f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, and

h\in\mathbb{L}_{0,2,2}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
hold.
Then there exists a unique Fourier-space weak solution
u
to (
2.2
) in the intersection of the classes

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.53325pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right),

where
\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)
denotes the smallest
\sigma
-algebras including all elements in
\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}
.

The proof of Theorem
3.11
will be given in Section
11
.

Remark 3.12
.

One might consider our data
u_{0}
and
f
in the theorem to be somewhat regular since they possess (spatial) realizable frequency functions that are locally integrable. However, the local integrability of the frequency function does not impose any restrictions on the behaviors at large frequencies. Consequently, the data could still be irregular due to the unrestricted nature at the large frequencies.

Remark 3.13
.

Formally, the solution
u
in Theorem
3.11
is expected to be

\displaystyle u(t,x)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Bigg[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[h(s,\cdot)\eta^{k}(\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\Bigg].

Then this type of solution could be considered as a mild solution.
However, this representation loses a mathematical rigor for a random symbol
\psi
, even though we developed new Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms.

For a deterministic symbol
\psi
, however, this mild formulation has a certain meaning due to the new Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms.
Nevertheless, even with the deterministic symbol
\psi
, the solution is generally not a complex-valued function.
Instead, it is a
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued stochastic process on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
, which is defined according to the action on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
as follows: for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)],\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left(\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}s\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[h(s,\cdot)\eta^{k}(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d}).

On the other hand, the Fourier transform of the solution
u
(with respect to the space variable) is a complex-valued function.
In this sense, we may say that our solution
u
is strong with respect to the time and frequencies of the space variable.

Remark 3.14
.

A similar theorem to Theorem
3.11
can also be obtained even if the stopping time

\tau
is allowed to be infinite at some points.
At first glance, it seems sufficient to consider the open interval
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$)$\kern-3.39996pt\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}}\,

instead of the half-interval
\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
in (
2.2
). However, we did not assume that our filtration

\mathscr{F}_{t}
is right-continuous, and additionally, the measure of the interval

\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$)$\kern-3.39996pt\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}}\,
could become infinite. Therefore, to develop this theory, we would need to find many appropriate versions and perform additional localization steps with respect to the time variable. We chose not to present this theorem in detail because these steps are typically tedious, and our settings are already sufficiently complicate.

If our Fourier transforms of data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
h
have certain finite (stochastic) moments, then we can derive certain stability results.
However, Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
are not enough to gain the stability results since our symbol is random.
Thus we introduce a stronger assumption.

Assumption 3.15

(Local boundedness on the symbol)
.

For all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{\infty}\left(\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}\right)}:=\esssup_{(\omega,t,\xi)\in\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left|\psi(t,\xi)\right|<\infty.

(3.10)

Remark 3.16
.

It is clear that Assumption
3.15
encompasses both Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
. This strong assumption is introduced to account for random symbols. Fortunately, the symbol for the fractional Laplacian operator with a complex exponent, as seen in Example
3.7
, continues to satisfy Assumption
3.15
. Moreover, we can even consider the fractional Laplacian operator with a random complex exponent, provided that the real part of the symbol is non-negative and uniformly bounded with respect to the sample points. Lastly, if we only consider non-random symbols, then Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
are sufficient to establish the stability result, which will be demonstrated in Section
8
.

Corollary 3.17
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
,
f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
, and

h\in\mathbb{L}_{1,2,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Suppose that
\psi
satisfies Assumption
3.15
.
Then there exists a unique Fourier-space weak solution
u
to (
2.2
) belonging to the intersection of the two classes

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,loc}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.53325pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\Big(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\Big).

Additionally, the solution
u
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\int_{B_{R}}|\psi(t,\xi)||\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\quad+R\cdot C_{BDG}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\|h(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\right]

(3.11)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau\wedge T]}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\quad+R\cdot C_{BDG}\cdot\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\|h(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\right]

(3.12)

for all
