R,T\in(0,\infty)
, where
\mathcal{C}^{i}_{R,T,\psi}

(i=1,2,3)
are positive constants which are explicitly given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}=\left[C_{R,T}^{\sup|\psi|}+2T\|\psi\|_{L_{\infty}\left(\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}\right)}\cdot C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}=C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}\left[1+2T\|\psi\|_{L_{\infty}\left(\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}\right)}C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}\right],

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}=1+\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}.

Remark 3.18
.

(
3.12
) clearly implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau\wedge T]}\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\quad+R\cdot C_{BDG}\cdot\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\|h(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\right],

which appears to be a more conventional form in estimates for SPDEs. However, it does not suffice to show

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,loc}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Remark 3.19
.

Generally, it is hard to determine which constant within
\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)
is smaller since they are derived from different methods.
Additionally, the constant
\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}
can be slightly smaller, which will be detailed in Remark
9.6
.
However, if our symbol
\psi
is non-random, then the constants
C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}
and
C_{R,T}^{\sup|\psi|}

can be replaced by some smaller constants in Assumptions
3.21
and
3.22
(or (
8.1
) and (
8.2
)).
This makes it possible for
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}
and
\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)
to be substituted with significantly simpler constants
C_{R,T}^{|\psi|}
and
C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}|\int\Re[\psi]|}
, respectively.
Additionally, for the deterministic symbol, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq T\cdot C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\quad+T\cdot C_{BDG}\cdot C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[g(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right],

where

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]}_{R,T}:=\esssup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T,\xi\in B_{R}}\left|\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right|<\infty.

These substitute constants seem to be optimal since they are directly obtained from the kernels consisting of
\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)
.
More details will be discussed in Corollary
8.10
.

Remark 3.20
.

Due to Plancherel’s theorem, it is evident that

\displaystyle\|h(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}=\|\mathcal{F}[h(t,\cdot)]\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}.

Thus (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) may indicate that the spatial Fourier transform of the solution
u
to (
2.2
) is governed by those of the data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
h
.
In other words, we could also say that
the frequency function (with respect to the space variable) of the solution
u
to (
2.2
) is stable under the perturbations of the data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
h
with respect to the spatial frequencies since our equation is linear.

We also present the result without the random noise component to compare assumptions on
\psi
.
Specifically, we consider the deterministic version of (
2.2
) as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u=\left(\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x)+f(t,x)\right)\mathrm{d}t,\quad

\displaystyle(t,x)\in\Omega\times(0,\tau)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},

\displaystyle u(0,x)=u_{0},\quad

\displaystyle x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}.

(3.13)

Equation (
3.13
) remains random because the symbol
\psi
, the inhomogeneous data
f
, and the initial data
u_{0}
could generally be random.
However, it is typically considered as a deterministic PDE since a solution can be easily obtained by solving the corresponding deterministic equation for each fixed sample point
\omega\in\Omega
.

This approach might raise concerns about whether the solution
u
, obtained by solving PDE for each
\omega\in\Omega
, is jointly measurable. Fortunately, when there is no stochastic (integral) term, joint measurability issues are minimal, making all mathematical conditions comparatively weaker. In particular, the conditions on
\psi
can be relaxed in the absence of a stochastic term.

Therefore, we also examine the well-posedness of (
3.13
) under weaker conditions in this section, which is presented as an independent theorem in Theorem
3.25
. We begin by proposing weaker assumptions on
\psi
.

Assumption 3.21
.

For all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}:=\esssup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T,\xi\in B_{R}}\left|\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right|<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Assumption 3.22
.

For all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle C^{|\psi|}_{R,T}:=\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}|\psi(t,\xi)|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\exp\left(\left|\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Remark 3.23
.

If the sign of
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]
is preserved,
i.e.

\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]\geq 0\quad\text{or}\quad\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]\leq 0\quad\forall t,\xi,

then the constant
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}
has both clear upper and lower bounds.
For instance, if
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]\leq 0
for all
t
and
\xi
, then

\displaystyle\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left|\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right|\leq C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}\leq 1.

This demonstrates that all constants associated with the symbol can be significantly simplified if the symbol meets an ellipticity condition.

Remark 3.24
.

It is clear that Assumptions
3.21
and
3.22
are less stringent than Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
as indicated by the inequalities

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}\leq C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}\quad(a.s.)

(3.14)

and

\displaystyle C^{|\psi|}_{R,T}\leq C^{\sup|\psi|}_{R,T}\quad(a.s.).

In other words, Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
are sufficient to ensure that Assumptions
3.21
and
3.22
are satisfied.
In particular,

C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}\leq C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}
and
C^{|\psi|}_{R,T}\leq C^{\sup|\psi|}_{R,T}

if the symbol is deterministic.
Note that the first equality is not generally true even though the symbol is deterministic.
Additionally, Assumption
3.21
is significant because this allows our main operators to encompass logarithmic operators, such as the logarithmic Laplacian (cf.
[
15
, Theorem 2.27]
).
Specifically, if
\psi(t,\xi)=\log|\xi|^{2}
, then
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}<\infty
but
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}=\infty
.

Moreover, the requirement in Assumption
3.22
can be somewhat relaxed to include logarithmic operators.
This will be further elaborated in Remark
9.3
.
In spite of this shortcoming, we opted for Assumption
3.22
over the weaker condition to maintain consistency with Assumption
3.2
since our primary focus is on SPDEs.
Notably, our results do not encompass (
2.2
) with logarithmic operators, and we firmly believe that theories involving logarithmic operators for SPDEs are not feasible due to intricate joint measurability issues, even though they are feasible for PDEs.

Lastly, if
\psi(t,\xi)
is deterministic and
\Re[\psi(t,\xi)]
is non-negative, then

C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}=C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}
.
This is why we use similar notations
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}_{R,T}
and
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}_{R,T}
even though the supremum with respect to the time variable is taken in different ways.

Theorem 3.25
.

Let

\displaystyle u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\quad\text{and}\quad f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

Suppose that
\psi
satisfies Assumption
3.21
and Assumption
3.22
.
Then there exists a unique Fourier-space weak solution
u
to (
3.13
) in the intersection of the two classes

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.53325pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right).

Moreover, the solution
u
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau\wedge T]}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\leq C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

(3.15)

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\int_{B_{R}}|\psi(t,\xi)||\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{R,T}^{|\psi|}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

(3.16)

with probability one.

The proof of Theorem
3.25
is given in Section
10
.

Remark 3.26
.

Formally, the kernel to (
3.13
) (or (
2.2
)) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)\right](x),\quad(0\leq s\leq t~\text{and}~x,\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d})

which serves as a solution to

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u=\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x)\mathrm{d}t,\quad

\displaystyle(t,x)\in\Omega\times(s,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},

\displaystyle u(s,x)=\delta_{0},\quad

\displaystyle x\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac delta centered at zero in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Thus it is important to control the functions
\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)
.
It seems that the imaginary part of these functions could be arbitrary since

\displaystyle\left|\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)\right|=\left|\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right|.

However, the imaginary part of
\psi(r,\xi)
must be integrable on any interval
(s,t)
to make the functions
\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)
well-defined even though these imaginary values do not affect estimates of the exponential functions. A sufficient condition to ensure the local integrability on
\Im[\psi(t,\xi)]
is provided by (
3.3
).

Recall that we assumed the weaker condition, Assumption
3.22
, than Assumption
3.2
in Theorem
3.25
.
However, (
3.3
) still can be derived from Assumption
3.22
.
Thus we did not independently add the condition (
3.3
) in Theorem
3.25
.

Remark 3.27
.

If the spatial Fourier transforms of our data
u_{0}
and
f
have finite moments, then that of the solution

u
(including those amplified by
\psi
) also has a finite moment, as both (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) hold almost surely. Additionally, (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) can be used to obtain various moment estimates of the solutions. For further details on these moment estimates, we refer to
[
37
]
.

4.
A well-posedness theory to SPDEs driven by white noises in time

In this section, we examine SPDEs driven by white noises in time as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u=\left(\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x)+f(t,x)\right)dt+g^{k}(t,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{t},\quad

\displaystyle(t,x)\in\Omega\times(0,\tau)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},

\displaystyle u(0,x)=u_{0},\quad

\displaystyle x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}.

(4.1)

Here
B_{t}^{k}

(k=1,2,\ldots)
are independent one-dimensional Brownian motions (Wiener processes) on
\Omega
,
\psi(t,\xi)
is a (complex-valued)
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
function defined on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
is a (time-measurable) pseudo-differential operator with the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
, i.e.

\displaystyle\psi(t,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(t,x):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\psi(t,\cdot)\mathcal{F}[u](t,\cdot)\right](x).

(4.2)

It is well-known that (
2.2
) is a special case of (
4.1
).
Consequently, there have been many attempts to study stochastic partial differential equations driven by space-time white noises
by using theories of corresponding SPDEs driven by a sequence of independent one-dimensional Brownian motions,
which is already mentioned before Definition
3.9
.
Therefore we introduce the definition of our weak solutions to (
4.1
), which includes Definition
3.9
.

Definition 4.1

(Fourier-space weak solution to (
4.1
))
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
,

\displaystyle f\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty));\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),\quad g\in\mathbb{L}_{0,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right),

and

u\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathscr{F}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty));\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right).

We say that
u
is a
Fourier-space weak solution
to equation (
4.1
) if for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u_{0},\varphi\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\psi(s,-\mathrm{i}\nabla)u(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle f(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle g^{k}(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.e.)\quad(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}.

(4.3)

Remark 4.2
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{L}_{0,2,loc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d};l_{2})\right)

and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.
Then for all
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|\langle g,\varphi\rangle\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Thus the stochastic integral term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left\langle g^{k}(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is well-defined for all
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)
.
In particular, for any
(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
, the term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle g^{k}(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}:=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left\langle g^{k}(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

in (
4.3
) is well-defined.
Moreover, if

g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,t,\xi}_{0,2,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right),

then the well-defined stochastic term is given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle g^{k}(s,\cdot),\varphi\right\rangle\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}[g^{k}(s,\cdot)](\xi)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\right)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

since for any
T\in(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}[g(s,\cdot)](\xi)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi\right|_{l_{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\right|\right)\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left(\int_{B_{R_{\varphi}}}|\mathcal{F}[g(s,\cdot)](\xi)|_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s<\infty\quad(a.s.),

where
{R_{\varphi}}
is a positive constant so that
\supp\mathcal{F}[\varphi]\subset B_{R_{\varphi}}
.
In particular, for any

g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right),

the stochastic term is well-defined due to the generalized Minkowski inequality.
Indeed, for any
R\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\left|\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[g(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right|_{l_{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\leq\int_{B_{R}}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau}\left|\mathcal{F}[g(t,\cdot)](\xi)\right|_{l_{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let

u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),\quad f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

and

g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right).

Suppose that
\psi
satisfies Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
.
Then there exists a unique Fourier-space weak solution
u
to (
4.1
) in the intersection of the two classes

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.53325pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right).

Remark 4.4
.

If
u_{0}
is
\mathscr{F}_{0}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable and
f
is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable then

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P},|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right).

This property is crucial for extending our results to treat multiplicative noise, which is an interesting direction for future research.

Remark 4.5
.

The class handling stochastic inhomogeneous data
g
is

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

in Theorem
4.3
.
This class is smaller than the class

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,t,\xi}_{0,2,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

mentioned in Remark
4.2
for defining the stochastic term in (
4.1
).
This restriction on the stochastic inhomogeneous data is important to applying stochastic Fubini theorems.
More specifics will be discussed later in upcoming sections.

Remark 4.6
.

Remark
3.13
can be reiterated in relation to Theorem
4.3
. The solution
u
in Theorem
4.3
is generally not a mild solution. However, in cases where
\psi
is deterministic, it can be considered as a mild solution within our new weak formulation.
In order to demonstrate this, assume that the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
is non-random.
Then the solution
u
in Theorem
4.3
is an
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued stochastic process defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
as follows: for any
(\omega,t)\in\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\mapsto\langle u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left(\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,\cdot)\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}s\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[g^{k}(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\right)\overline{\mathcal{F}[\varphi](\xi)}\mathrm{d}\xi.

(4.4)

It is important to note that the function
u
in (
4.4
) is not defined as a complex-valued function pointwisely.
However, the Fourier transform of
u
with respect to the space variable is a complex-valued function
(a.e.)
defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}

based on the canonical identification with a locally integrable function and a distribution. In other words,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,\cdot)\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[g^{k}(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s},

(4.5)

where all terms in the right-hand side are obviously complex-valued functions
(a.e.)
defined on
\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
due to the assumptions in Theorem
4.3
.
Thus we may say that our solution has a strong or realizable frequency function (with respect to the space variable).

Moreover, observe that all functions appearing in (
4.5
) are locally integrable, which means they can be regarded as distributions.
However, they generally do not qualify as tempered distributions because of insufficient conditions on the data
u_{0}
,
f
, and
g
.
Hence
u
cannot be derived from (
4.5
) by taking the classical inverse Fourier transform applicable for tempered distributions.
However, our new inverse Fourier transform, as defined in Definition
2.3
, enables us to recover
u
from (
4.5
) as
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued stochastic processes by applying it with respect to the spatial frequencies.
This idea is the starting point to find a unique solution
u
to (
4.1
) rigorously.

Remark 4.7
.

It is readily observable that the right-hand side of (
4.4
) is defined for all
t\in(0,\infty)
, which implies that the solution
u
can nicely be extended to
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
More precisely, there exists a
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued stochastic process
\tilde{u}
on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
such that for all
(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}
,

\tilde{u}(\omega,t)=u(\omega,t)
as
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-valued functions.
Additionally,
\tilde{u}
belongs to the intersection of the classes

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{loc}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,\infty),\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right),

where

\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{loc}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,\infty),\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

\displaystyle\iff u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,T],\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty)

and

\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times(0,T]\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

These extensions will be revisited in later sections to demonstrate the existence of solutions with deterministic symbols.

It is naturally anticipated that the solution
u
has a finite expectation if the data have finite expectations. This rule also applies to their spatial Fourier transforms, as previously shown in Corollary
3.17
. A similar result can be established for the relationship between the solution and the data in (
4.1
).
Moreover, the solution mapping
(u_{0},f,g)\mapsto u
is continuous in terms of the spatial Fourier transforms.
However, for the stability result to encompass random symbols,
\psi
should satisfy a stronger condition, as emphasized in Corollary
3.17
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let

\displaystyle u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),\quad f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

and

g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right).

Suppose that
\psi
satisfies Assumption
3.15
.
Then there exists a unique Fourier-space weak solution
u
to (
4.1
) in the intersection of the two classes

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,loc}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.53325pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,loc,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right).

Moreover, the solution
u
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\int_{B_{R}}|\psi(t,\xi)||\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{BDG}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[g(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

(4.6)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau\wedge T]}|\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}|\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\xi\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{BDG}\cdot\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge T}\left|\mathcal{F}[g(s,\cdot)](\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right],

(4.7)

for all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
, where
\mathcal{C}^{i}_{R,T,\psi}

(i=1,2,3)
are positive constants which are explicitly given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}=\left[C_{R,T}^{\sup|\psi|}+2T\|\psi\|_{L_{\infty}\left(\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}\right)}\cdot C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}=C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}\left[1+2T\|\psi\|_{L_{\infty}\left(\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}\right)}C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\psi]|}\right],

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}=1+\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}.

The proofs of Theorem
4.3
and Corollary
4.8
are given in Section
10
.

Remark 4.9
.

\mathcal{C}^{1}_{R,T,\psi}
and
\left(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{R,T,\psi}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{3}_{R,T,\psi}\right)
can be optimized to
C_{R,T}^{|\psi|}
and
C_{R,T}^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\psi]}
if the symbol
\psi
is non-random as discussed in Remark
3.19
.

Remark 4.10
.

Observe that the constants in (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) strongly depend on each
T
.
Thus there is no guarantee that the solution
u
belongs to the smaller spaces such as

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1}\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\text{$[$\kern-1.49994pt$[$}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

and

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\sigma\left(\mathscr{G}\times\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\cup\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d}),|\psi(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right)

even if our data satisfy the better conditions that

f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{1,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}\right)

and

g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathbb{L}^{\omega,\xi,t}_{1,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})|_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}};l_{2}\right).

It is because the BDG inequality does not perfectly work for our solution
u
, which is discussed further in Remark
6.9
.

5.
Stochastic Fubini theorems

Our estimates can be derived using approximations by simple stochastic processes, such as step processes. However, obtaining rigorous theories through these approximations is challenging since our solutions and data generally lack finite moments. Instead, it is more effective to directly estimate many stochastic terms using the stochastic Fubini theorems.

Recent advancements in the stochastic Fubini theorems (
cf.

[
55
,
35
,
63
]
) have been made.
We rigorously develop our theories with the help of these theorems. Here, we introduce a version of the stochastic Fubini theorem that is well-suited to our applications.

Theorem 5.1

(Stochastic Fubini theorem)
.

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
,
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
,

F\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

and
G\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
.
Assume that
|A|<\infty
,

\displaystyle\int_{A}\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(t)\left|F(\omega,t,x)\right|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.)

and

\displaystyle\int_{A}\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(t)\left|G(\omega,t,x)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.),

where
|A|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
A
.
Then there exists a
\sigma\left(\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P}\right)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})|_{\Omega\times[0,T]\times A}
-measurable function
H(\omega,t,x)
on
\Omega\times[0,T]\times A
such that

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{A}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T]

(ii)

\displaystyle\int_{A}H(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{A}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{A}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

where the series
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{A}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
converges in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
.

(iii)

for almost every
x\in A
,

H(\omega,t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

where the series
\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
and
\int_{A}\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\mathrm{d}x
converge in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
.

In particular,

\displaystyle\int_{A}\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}x+\int_{A}\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{A}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{A}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T]

by identifying
H(\omega,t,x)
and
\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
based on the equivalent relation that is equal
(a.e.)
on
\Omega\times[0,T]\times A
.
Moreover, if
F
is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable, then
(\omega,t,x)\mapsto 1_{[0,T]}(t)1_{A}(x)H(\omega,t,x)
is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.

Proof.

The assumptions

F\in\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times A,\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

and

G\in\mathbb{L}_{0,0,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times A,\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)

imply that the extensions

\displaystyle 1_{(0,T]}(t)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}\left(\omega,t\right)F(\omega,t,x):=\begin{cases}&F(\omega,t,x)\quad\text{if~ $(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\wedge T\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}$ }\\
&0\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle 1_{(0,T]}(t)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}\left(\omega,t\right)G(\omega,t,x):=\begin{cases}&G(\omega,t,x)\quad\text{if~ $(\omega,t)\in\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\wedge T\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}$ }\\
&0\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

are
\sigma(\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P})\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})|_{\Omega\times[0,T]\times A}
-measurable and

\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})|_{\Omega\times[0,T]\times A}
-measurable, respectively.

Then, the theorem can be derived straightforwardly from a version of the stochastic Fubini theorem. For further details, refer to
[
55
, Theorem 3.5]
,
[
35
, Lemmas 2.6 and 2.7]
, and
[
63
, Theorem 2.2]
, which discuss stochastic Fubini theorems in a general context sufficient to encompass our theorem.
However, we acknowledge that our theorem cannot be directly obtained from the aforementioned results.
Some suitable adjustments need to be made by inspecting their proofs.
∎

Put
A=B_{R}
with
R\in(0,\infty)
in Theorem
5.1
and recall the condition that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\left|G(\omega,t,x)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.).

(5.1)

Then this condition ensures that the stochastic term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\wedge T\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is well-defined for all
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T]
due to the generalized Minkowski inequality:

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left|\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left|1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\wedge T\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle<\infty\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in(0,\infty).

Additionally, (
5.1
) implies that for almost every
x\in B_{R}
,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\left|G(\omega,t,x)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Thus for almost every
x\in B_{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is well-defined for all
(\omega,t)\in[0,T]
and is continuous with respect to
t
.
In other words, the stochastic integral

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is a complex-valued function defined
(a.e.)
on
\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
so that for almost every
x\in B_{R}
and
\omega\in\Omega
,
the mapping

\displaystyle t\in[0,T]\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is continuous.
Due to this construction, for almost every
x\in B_{R}
, the mapping

\displaystyle(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\mapsto 1_{[0,T]}(t)\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

is
\mathcal{P}
-measurable.
However, the joint measurability of

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

cannot be established directly in this way.
Fortunately, the stochastic Fubini theorem ensures that there exists a modification of

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

which is joint measurable on
\Omega\times[0,T]\times A
.

Next consider another version of the stochastic Fubini theorem which is uniformly given for all
T\in(0,\infty)

by summing the localizations.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
F\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and

G\in\mathbb{L}^{\omega,x,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right).

Then there exists a
\sigma(\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{P})\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function
H(\omega,t,x)
on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that

(i)

for each
R\in(0,\infty)
and
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.),

(ii)

for each
R\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}H(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,\infty),

the series
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
converges in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
for all
T\in(0,\infty)
.

(iii)

for almost every
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle H(\omega,t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,\infty),

where the series
\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
converges in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
for all
T\in(0,\infty)
and additionally for any
R\in(0,\infty)
, the series
\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
converges in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
for all
T\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

It may seem that this theorem can also be obtained from a general version of the stochastic Fubini theorem since the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
is
\sigma
-finite. However, we could not find an appropriate reference and thus suggest a proof with detail by applying Theorem
5.1
.

It is sufficient to find a
\sigma(\mathscr{H}\times\mathcal{P})\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function
H(\omega,t,x)
on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that for all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.),

(5.2)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}H(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(5.3)

and for almost every
x\in B_{R}
,

\displaystyle H(\omega,t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(5.4)

where all the series (including the series
\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}
) converge in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
.
Recall the condition that
F\in\mathbb{L}_{0,1,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)
and

G\in\mathbb{L}^{\omega,x,t}_{0,2,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right).

Then for all
T,R\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\left|F(\omega,t,x)\right|\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x+\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(t)\left|G(\omega,t,x)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Thus by Theorem
5.1
, for each
R,T\in(0,\infty)
, there exists a
\sigma(\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{P})\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})|_{\Omega\times[0,T]\times B_{R}}
-measurable function
H_{T,R}
on
\Omega\times[0,T]\times B_{R}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in T}|H_{T,R}(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.),

(5.5)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}H_{T,R}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

and for almost every
x\in B_{R}
,

\displaystyle H_{T,R}(\omega,t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(5.6)

where all the series converge in probability uniformly on
[0,T]
.

Next we claim that for all
T\in(0,\infty)
and
0<R_{1}\leq R\leq R_{2}<\infty
,

\displaystyle H_{T,R_{1}}=H_{T,R}=H_{T,R_{2}}\quad(a.e.)~\text{on}~\Omega\times[0,T]\times B_{R_{1}}

(5.7)

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}\setminus B_{R_{1}}}H_{T,R_{2}}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}\setminus B_{R_{1}}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}\setminus B_{R_{1}}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

(5.8)

Due to (
5.6
) and the Fubini theorem, it is nearly evident that (
5.7
) holds.
To prove (
5.8
), we use (
5.7
) and the linearity of the integrations.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}\setminus B_{R_{1}}}H_{T,R_{2}}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}H_{T,R_{2}}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{B_{R_{1}}}H_{T,R_{2}}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}H_{T,R}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{B_{R_{1}}}H_{T,R_{1}}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}\setminus B_{R_{1}}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}\setminus B_{R_{1}}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

Here we used the fact that all paths are continuous on
[0,T]
almost surely.
From this, we can construct the function
H
as follows:

\displaystyle H(\omega,t,x):=\begin{cases}&0\quad\text{if}~t=0\\
&H_{n,m}(\omega,t,x)\quad\text{if}~(\omega,t,x)\in\Omega\times(n-1,n]\times B_{m}\setminus B_{m-1}.\end{cases}

Then it is obvious that
H
is a
\sigma(\mathscr{H}\cup\mathcal{P})\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function function defined on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
We now show that
H
satisfies (
5.2
), (
5.3
), and (
5.4
).
Let
T,R\in(0,\infty)
.
Denote by
\lceil T\rceil
and
\lceil R\rceil
the smallest integers such that

T\leq\lceil T\rceil
and
R\leq\lceil R\rceil
, respectively.
Then first by (
5.5
),

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in T}|H(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\lceil R\rceil}\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil T\rceil}\int_{B_{R}}1_{B_{m}\setminus B_{m-1}}(x)\sup_{t\in T}|H_{n,m}(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\lceil R\rceil}\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil T\rceil}\int_{B_{m}}\sup_{t\in T}|H_{n,m}(\omega,t,x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Next by (
5.8
),

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}H(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\lceil R\rceil}\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil T\rceil}1_{(n-1,n]}(t)\int_{B_{R}}1_{B_{m}\setminus B_{m-1}}(x)H_{n,m}(\omega,t,x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B_{R}}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

Finally, by (
5.7
) and (
5.6
), for almost every
x\in B_{R}\subset B_{\lceil R\rceil}
,

\displaystyle H(\omega,t,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\lceil R\rceil}\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil T\rceil}1_{(n-1,n]}(t)1_{B_{m}\setminus B_{m-1}}(x)H_{n,m}(\omega,t,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil T\rceil}1_{(n-1,n]}(t)H_{n,\lceil R\rceil}(\omega,t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)F(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(\omega,s,x)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}\quad(a.s.)\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

The corollary is proved.
∎

6.
Stochastic Fubini theorems with deterministic symbols

In this section, we apply the stochastic Fubini theorems developed in the previous section to establish our existence result.
Recall that the solution
u
to (
4.1
) is given so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u(t,\cdot)](\xi)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,\cdot)\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathcal{F}[u_{0}](\xi)+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[f(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[g^{k}(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s},

(6.1)

if the symbol
\psi
is deterministic, which is mentioned in Remark
4.6
.
Consequently, our solution
u
will be derived from the above relation if the symbol is non-random.
Here, we provide more details explaining why this works only for deterministic symbols.

It is evident that the most challenging part to handle in (
6.1
) is the stochastic (integral) term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\mathcal{F}[g^{k}(s,\cdot)](\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}.

(6.2)

Firstly, it is essential to verify whether the stochastic term is well-defined based on our main assumptions and the stochastic Fubini theorems. Once this is established, a solution
u
could be derived from the relation (
6.1
) with a certain mathematical meaning.
However, for each
t\in(0,\infty)
and
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the mapping

\displaystyle(\omega,s)\mapsto\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\cdot 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\wedge t\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)

(6.3)

is not predictable anymore since the term
\int_{s}^{t}\psi(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r
is always merely
\mathscr{F}_{t}
-adapted for any
s\in(0,t)
in general. In other words, the term in (
6.3
) is not predictable (even not progressive measurable) since it is not
\mathscr{F}_{s}
-adapted for each
s
.
Therefore, (
6.2
) cannot be defined as an Itô stochastic integral in general unless there is a very strong assumption regarding the randomness of
\psi
to make the term in (
6.3
) predictable (or progressive measurable).

Nevertheless, the term in (
6.3
) naturally arises when seeking an appropriate candidate for a solution to (
4.1
), as demonstrated in (
6.1
). This indicates that our existence theory cannot be directly derived from the stochastic Fubini theorems unless the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
meets a very strong assumption regarding the sample point
\omega
, due to the predictability issue mentioned earlier.

As a first step to ensure that (
6.3
) is predictable and employ (
6.1
), we specifically assume that the symbol
\psi(t,\xi)
is deterministic.
This should be regarded as the simplest form of randomness.

Additionally, we use the notation
\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)
in order to emphasize that the symbol
\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)
is deterministic.
In other words, we assume that our symbol
\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)
is non-random,
i.e.

\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)
is a complex-valued
\mathcal{B}([0,\infty)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function defined on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
throughout the section.

Moreover, suppose that our symbol
\tilde{\psi}
satisfies weaker variants of Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
so that for all
R,T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{e}|\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]|}_{R,T}:=\esssup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T,\xi\in B_{R}}\left|\exp\left(\left|\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\right|<\infty

(6.4)

and

\displaystyle C^{|\tilde{\psi}|}_{R,T}:=\esssup_{\xi\in B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}|\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\exp\left(\left|\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\mathrm{d}t\right)<\infty.

(6.5)

One can view
\tilde{\psi}
as a complex-valued
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
by considering the canonical constant extension that
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)
.
Then the condition in (
6.4
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\esssup_{(\omega,t,\xi)\in\Omega\times(0,T)\times B_{R}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\Re[\psi(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|<\infty,

which shows that this condition is less restrictive than Assumption
3.1
.
In particular, the constant
C^{\mathrm{e}|\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]|}_{R,T}
is located between
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]}_{R,T}
and
C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\tilde{\psi}]|}_{R,T}
,
i.e.

C^{\mathrm{e}\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]}_{R,T}\leq C^{\mathrm{e}|\int\Re[\tilde{\psi}]|}_{R,T}\leq C^{\mathrm{e}\int\sup|\Re[\tilde{\psi}]|}_{R,T}
, where the other notations came from Assumption
3.1
and Assumption
3.21
. Moreover, by recalling Assumption
3.2
, it is obvious that

\displaystyle C^{|\tilde{\psi}|}_{R,T}\leq C^{\sup|\tilde{\psi}|}_{R,T},

which implies that (
6.5
) is slightly weaker.

Now we connect our main assumptions on the symbol with the stochastic Fubini theorems from the previous section. We present several corollaries of the stochastic Fubini theorems.
The conditions (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) on the symbol
\tilde{\psi}
are considered separately in the following corollaries except the last one, and even some weaker conditions on

\tilde{\psi}
are introduced.
Additionally, we explore natural extensions of the stochastic integrals to the entire space

\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
with appropriate new classes.

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
G\in\mathbb{L}^{\omega,x,t}_{0,1,2,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
.
Suppose that (
6.4
) holds and for almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|\tilde{\psi}(s,\xi)|\mathrm{d}s<\infty\quad\forall t\in(0,\infty).

(6.6)

Denote

\displaystyle H(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(s,\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}.

(6.7)

Then

\displaystyle H\in\mathbb{C}_{loc}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right),

where

\displaystyle H\in\mathbb{C}_{loc}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

\displaystyle\iff H\in\mathbb{C}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

Proof.

First we show that
H
is a complex-valued
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function defined on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Put

\displaystyle H_{1}(t,\xi)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)

and

\displaystyle H_{2}(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(s,\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}.

Then obviously
H_{1}
is well-defined due to (
6.6
).
Additionally, since the symbol
\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)
is non-random and
\mathcal{B}([0,\infty)\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable,
it is easy to show that the mapping

(\omega,t,\xi)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\in\mathbf{C}

is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.
On the other hand, applying (
6.4
) and recalling the condition

G\in\mathbb{L}^{\omega,x,t}_{0,2,1,\ell oc}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.39996pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.49994pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right),

for all
T,R\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left|\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq\esssup_{s\in(0,T),\xi\in B_{R}}\exp\left(\left|\int_{0}^{s}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left|1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.).

Thus by Corollary
5.2
,

H_{2}(t,\xi)
is also
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable (by considering the modification in the corollary) and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H_{2}(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\quad(a.s.).

(6.8)

Therefore
H(t,\xi)=H_{1}(t,\xi)H_{2}(t,\xi)
is a
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable function defined on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Next, we claim

\displaystyle H\in\mathbb{C}_{loc}L_{1,\ell oc}\left(\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\right)

to complete the proof.
First, it is obvious that both
H_{1}(t,\xi)
and
H_{2}(t,\xi)
are continuous with respect to
t
for almost every
\xi
and
\omega
.
Putting

\displaystyle M=\esssup_{(t,\xi)\in[0,T]\times B_{R}}\left|\exp\left(\left|\int_{0}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\right|

and using (
6.4
) and (
6.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H(t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\leq M\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H_{2}(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\quad(a.s.).

(6.9)

Additionally, applying the stochastic Fubini theorem, a well-known property of the Itô stochastic integral (
cf.
[
33
, Theorem 6.3.5]
and
[
35
, (2.5)]
), and the generalized Minkowski inequality, for all
\varepsilon,\delta,T,R\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H_{2}(t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\geq\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\left|\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]^{2}\geq\delta\right)+\frac{\delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

(6.10)

By combining (
6.10
) and (
6.9
),

\displaystyle P\left(\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H(t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\geq\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H_{2}(t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{M}\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\left|\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]^{2}\geq\delta\right)+M^{2}\frac{\delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Taking
\varepsilon\to\infty
and
\delta\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H(t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi=\infty\right)=0\quad\forall T,R\in(0,\infty).

Since the
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurability had already been shown above, the corollary is proved.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Generally, (
6.4
) does not imply (
6.6
) because it only controls the real part of
\psi
. However, (
6.5
) does imply (
6.6
), which will be used later to apply Corollary
6.1
with the symbol satisfying both (
6.4
) and (
6.5
).
Moreover, it is possible to construct our theories with the symbol satisfying (
6.4
), (
6.6
), and a slightly weaker condition than (
6.5
), which will be revisited in Remark
6.13
.

Next, we impose a finite expected condition on the product of the
\exp\left(\tilde{\psi}\right)
and data
G
.
We assume a slightly weaker condition on the symbol
\tilde{\psi}
rather than (
6.4
).
This weaker assumption still enables us to estimate
H
in terms of
\tilde{\psi}
and
G
.
Additionally, recall that (
6.4
) is used to show the joint measurability of
H
in the proof of Corollary
6.1
.
This demonstrates that (
6.4
) is sufficiently given not only for obtaining estimates but also for ensuring joint measurability. In other words, the condition on
\tilde{\psi}
seems to be possible to be weakened if one just focuses on estimates.
However, without joint measurability, the rigor of all theories falls apart.
In this context, we strongly believe that the condition in the following corollary cannot replace
(
6.4
)
in our main theorems even with deterministic symbols.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
G\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
.
Suppose that (
6.6
) holds and for almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{T}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

(6.11)

Denote

\displaystyle H(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(s,\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}.

Then
H
is defined (a.e.) on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|H(t,\xi)|\right]\leq C_{BDG}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}2\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\left|1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

(6.12)

for almost every
(t,\xi)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

We show that for each
t
,
H(t,\xi)
is well-defined as a random variable for almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
Fix
t\in[0,\infty)
.
By applying (
6.11
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left|\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}ds\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]<\infty\quad(a.e.)~\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}.

(6.13)

Additionally, by utilizing the Fubini theorem, it is possible to observe that for almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, both the mappings
(\omega,s)\mapsto 1_{s\leq t}\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)\mathrm{d}r
and
(\omega,s)\mapsto 1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)
are
\mathcal{P}
-measurable.
Fix
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
so that (
6.13
) and the above measurability conditions hold.
Then, the mapping

\displaystyle(\omega,s)\mapsto 1_{s\leq t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)

is
\mathcal{P}
-measurable.
Therefore, for almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the stochastic integral
H(t,\xi)
is well-defined as a random variable (for each fixed
t
).
Additionally, by the BDG inequality, we have (
6.12
).
The corollary is proved.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

(
6.11
) itself is not enough to guarantee that
H
is joint measurable since the stochastic integral
H(t,\xi)
in Corollary
6.3
is defined in an iterated way.
Even the stronger condition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{T}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall T,R\in(0,\infty)

is not sufficient to ensure that
H
is joint-measurable.
We suggest an efficient condition to make
H
joint-measurable in the next corollary.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
G\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
.
Suppose that (
6.6
) holds and for any
R\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

(6.14)

Denote

\displaystyle H(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(s,\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}.

Then
H
is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable, (
6.12
) holds, and for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
H
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H(t,\xi)|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{BDG}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle=C_{BDG}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right).

(6.15)

Proof.

It is obvious that (
6.6
) and (
6.14
) imply (
6.11
) by elementary measure theories with the Fubini theorem.
Thus due to Corollary
6.3
,
it suffices to show that
H(t,\xi)
is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable and (
6.15
) holds.
We split
H
into two parts

\displaystyle H_{1}(t,\xi)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)

and

\displaystyle H_{2}(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)G(s,\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}

so that
H(t,\xi)=H_{1}(t,\xi)H_{2}(t,\xi)
.
Then by (
6.6
),
H_{1}(t,\xi)
is well-defined for all
t\in[0,\infty)
and almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
On the other hand, (
6.14
) with the Fubini theorem implies that for any
t\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]<\infty\quad(a.e.)\quad\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}.

(6.16)

Thus for any
t\in(0,\infty)
,
H_{2}(t,\xi)
is well-defined as a random variable for almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}

since the predictability of the integrand can be easily shown as in the proof of Corollary
6.1
.
Moreover, it is straightforward to demonstrate that both functions
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto H_{1}(t,\xi)
and
(\omega,t,\xi)\mapsto H_{2}(t,\xi)

are
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable (by considering a modification) due to the stochastic Fubini theorem and continuity of paths.
This directly implies that
H
is
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
-measurable.

Next we show (
6.15
). We estimate
H_{2}
first.
By the BDG inequality, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and almost every
\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H_{2}(t,\xi)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{BDG}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H(t,\xi)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H_{1}(t,\xi)|\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|H_{2}(t,\xi)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{BDG}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right],

which completes (
6.15
).
∎

Remark 6.6
.

Recall the term

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right),

from (
6.15
).
Here sup is taken for all
t\in[0,T]
, and the integration is also taken for all
s\in[0,T]
.
Thus the term
\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r
is defined as

\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r=-\int_{t}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\quad\text{for $t\leq s$}

according to the convention of the notation for integrals.
Therefore, the inequality

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right)

is not generally true.
Instead, the inequality

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T}\left[\exp\left(\left|\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right|\right)\right]\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.71053pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.19579pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right)

seems to be optimal to include all sign-changing symbols.

Next, we prepare to estimate the product of the symbol and the spatial Fourier transform of a solution.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
G\in\mathbb{L}_{0}\left(\text{\,\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-3.4755pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.53325pt$]$}\times\mathbf{R}^{d},\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{R}^{d});l_{2}\right)
.
Suppose that (
6.6
) holds and for any
R\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{s}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty),

(6.17)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{T}|\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)|\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty\quad\forall T\in(0,\infty).

(6.18)

Denote

\displaystyle H(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)G^{k}(s,\xi)\mathrm{d}B^{k}_{s}.

Then
H
satisfies (
6.12
), (
6.15
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{R}}|\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)||H(t,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{BDG}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\int_{0}^{T}|\tilde{\psi}(t,\xi)|\left(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[2\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\xi\right]<\infty,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{R}}\left|H(t,\xi)\right|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq T\cdot C_{BDG}\int_{B_{R}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T}\left|\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\Re[\tilde{\psi}(r,\xi)]\mathrm{d}r\right)\right|\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}1_{\text{\,\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{${\scriptstyle|}$}\kern-2.87111pt$($}0,\tau\text{$]$\kern-1.26662pt$]$}}(s)\left|G(s,\xi)\right|^{2}_{l_{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\mathrm{d}\xi\right),

(6.20)

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|H(t,\xi)|\right]\mathrm{d}\xi
